dependencies or semantic similarities across the input sequence.
The overall architecture is depicted in Figure 9-7, which shows the flow of data through the layers of the decoder
and how the attention mechanisms and feedforward networks are structured.
Figure 9-7. Structure of a decoder-only transformer architecture
There are many variations on this basic architecture, including modifications to the attention mechanism (e.g.,
sparse attention, linear attention), changes to the feedforward network (e.g., using different activation functions or
adding additional layers), and adjustments to the overall structure (e.g., adding more layers, changing the size of
the embeddings). These variations are beyond the scope of this book, but the key point is that the decoder-only
architecture has become the standard for LLMs due to its effectiveness in generating coherent and contextually
relevant text.
Revisiting the MNIST Image Classification Example
While the MNIST image classification example we covered earlier in this chapter used a CNN, it is also possible
to apply transformer architectures to image classification tasks. Using the same image data as for CNNs, we can
train a simple transformer model for image classification. Import the necessary libraries and set the key parameters
in the model:
import torch
from torch import nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
device = torch.device(
"cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else
"mps" if torch.backends.mps.is_available() else
"cpu")
IMG_SIZE = 28
PATCH_SIZE = 4
IN_CHANNELS = 1
NUM_CLASSES = 10 # ten digits (0-9)
EMBED_DIM = 64 # every token is a vector of 64
NUM_HEADS = 4 # number of attention heads running in parallel
NUM_LAYERS = 3 # number of transformer layers
MLP_DIM = 128
DROPOUT = 0.1 # during TRAINING randomly set 10% of values to zero
The raw patch size is 4 × 4 pixels, but after flattening it becomes 16 (4 * 4).
After attention, each token passes through a neural network with a hidden layer twice the size of the
embedding dimension (128 versus 64).
Define the components of the transformer model for image classification. The model consists of a patch projection
layer, learnable tokens and positional encoding, a dropout layer, transformer encoder layers, a normalization layer,
and a final multilayer perceptron (MLP) head for classification:
class VisionTransformer(nn.Module):
def __init__(self, img_size=IMG_SIZE, patch_size=PATCH_SIZE,
in_channels=IN_CHANNELS, num_classes=NUM_CLASSES,
embed_dim=EMBED_DIM, num_heads=NUM_HEADS,
num_layers=NUM_LAYERS, mlp_dim=MLP_DIM,
dropout=DROPOUT):
super().__init__()
# ── Patch Projection ──
self.patch_projection = nn.Conv2d(
in_channels, embed_dim,
kernel_size=patch_size, stride=patch_size)
num_patches = (img_size // patch_size) ** 2
# ── Learnable Tokens & Positional Encoding ──
self.cls_token = nn.Parameter(torch.zeros(1, 1, embed_dim))
self.pos_embedding = nn.Parameter(
torch.zeros(1, num_patches + 1, embed_dim))
self._reset_parameters()
self.dropout = nn.Dropout(dropout)
# ── Transformer Encoder ──
encoder_layer = nn.TransformerEncoderLayer(
d_model=embed_dim,
nhead=num_heads,
dim_feedforward=mlp_dim,
dropout=dropout,
batch_first=True,
activation="gelu",
)
self.transformer = nn.TransformerEncoder(
encoder_layer, num_layers=num_layers)
# ── Norm Layer ──
self.norm = nn.LayerNorm(embed_dim)
# ── MLP Layer for Classification ──
self.mlp_head = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(embed_dim, mlp_dim), nn.GELU(),
nn.Dropout(dropout), nn.Linear(mlp_dim, num_classes))
def _reset_parameters(self):
nn.init.trunc_normal_(self.cls_token, std=0.02)
nn.init.trunc_normal_(self.pos_embedding, std=0.02)
def forward(self, images):
B = images.shape[0]
# ── Step 1–2: Extract patches and project to embed_dim
# (B, 1, 28, 28) → (B, 64, 7, 7) → (B, 49, 64)
x = self.patch_projection(images) # (B, EMBED_DIM, 7, 7)
x = x.flatten(2).transpose(1, 2) # (B, NUM_PATCHES, EMBED_DIM)
# ── Step 3: Prepend CLS token ──
# (B, 49, 64) → (B, 50, 64)
cls_tokens = self.cls_token.expand(B, -1, -1)
x = torch.cat([cls_tokens, x], dim=1)
# ── Step 4: Add positional encoding
x = x + self.pos_embedding
x = self.dropout(x)
# ── Step 5: Transformer encoder ──
x = self.transformer(x)
# ── Step 6: CLS token → MLP head ──
cls_output = self.norm(x[:, 0]) # (B, 64)
return self.mlp_head(cls_output) # (B, 10)
model = VisionTransformer().to(device)
The 64 dimensions are split into 4 heads, each looking for a different feature; each head works on 64 / 4 = 16
dimensions.
Instead of starting with all zeros, the tokens and embeddings start out with small random values (to avoid
exploding/vanishing gradients).
A raw patch from a 4 × 4 pixel region is just 16 numbers (1 channel × 4 × 4). This is too small and not rich
enough for the transformer to work with, so the projection stretches each patch from 16 → 64 dimensions.
After patch projection, we have 49 patch vectors.
In the transformer layers, the CLS token asks each patch: “What information do you have?” and collects this
information. Position embedding adds a unique “location tag” to each patch.
A dropout layer is added during training to randomly set 10% of the values to zero, which helps prevent
overfitting by encouraging the model to learn more robust features.
The normalization layer ensures each token has a mean of 0 and standard deviation of 1.
The final layer takes the CLS token (a vector of 64 numbers) and converts it into 10 class scores.
print(f"Total Parameters: {sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()):>8,}")
---
Total: 114,506
The total number of parameters is 114,506 with 87% in the transformer layers, 10% in the MLP head, and 3% in
the patch projection and learnable tokens/embeddings.
The forward path has six steps:
1. Split image into patches
2. Project patches to embed_dim
3. Prepend CLS token
4. Add positional encoding
5. Pass through transformer encoder
6. Extract CLS token → MLP head → class scores
Train the model by iterating over five epochs. We make use of the functions train_one_epoch and
evaluate that we defined in the CNN example:
optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.001)
criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss()
num_epochs = 5
for epoch in range(num_epochs):
avg_loss = train_one_epoch(model, trainloader, optimizer, criterion)
accuracy = evaluate(model, testloader)
print(
f"Epoch [{epoch+1}/{num_epochs}] "
f"Loss: {avg_loss:.4f} "
f"Test Accuracy: {accuracy:.2f}%"
)
---
Epoch [1/5] Loss: 0.8796 Test Accuracy: 92.35%
Epoch [2/5] Loss: 0.2605 Test Accuracy: 95.97%
Epoch [3/5] Loss: 0.1898 Test Accuracy: 95.95%
Epoch [4/5] Loss: 0.1550 Test Accuracy: 96.55%
Epoch [5/5] Loss: 0.1342 Test Accuracy: 97.07%
The accuracy of the transformer model is almost the same as the CNN model.
Apply the model to predict the same image as in the CNN model:
sample_image, label = testset[160]
pred, probs = predict(model, sample_image)
print(f"Predicted class: {pred} Actual class: {label}")
print(", ".join([f"{p:.3f}" for p in probs.tolist()]))
---
Predicted class: 4 Actual class: 4
0.000, 0.000, 0.000, 0.000, 0.998, 0.000, 0.000, 0.001, 0.000, 0.000
In contrast to the CNN model, the transformer model predicts the correct class (4) for this image and it is confident
in its prediction (99.8% probability for class 4). While both models achieve similar overall accuracy, the
transformer model’s prediction for this specific image is correct, illustrating that different architectures may have
different strengths and weaknesses in terms of which examples they classify correctly or incorrectly.
Like the CNN example, this example also illustrates the complexity and black box nature of deep learning models.
There are more than 114,000 parameters to be trained in this relatively simple example. Transformer models would
typically have many more layers and have 5 to 150 million parameters.
KEY IDEAS
Transformers are a type of deep learning architecture that relies on attention mechanisms to capture
relationships and dependencies in data, particularly effective for natural language processing tasks.
Transformers are used in all modern LLMs such as GPT, Claude, and Gemini.
Key components of transformers include embeddings, attention mechanisms, and multi-head
attention, which allow the model to learn complex patterns and relationships in the data.
Transformers were made possible by the availability of large data sets and powerful computing
resources (especially GPUs), enabling the training of models with billions or even trillions of
parameters.
Further Reading
The seminal paper “Attention is All You Need” that introduced the transformer architecture is a must-read
for anyone interested in understanding the foundations of modern LLMs. It provides a detailed
explanation of the attention mechanism and how it enables the model to capture long-range dependencies
in data.
Hands-On Generative AI with Transformers and Diffusion Models by Omar Sanseviero, Pedro Cuenca,
Apolinário Passos, and Jonathan Whitaker (O’Reilly, 2024) provides a practical introduction to
transformer architectures and their applications in generative AI, including hands-on examples and code
snippets.
Build a Large Language Model (From Scratch) by Sebastian Raschka (Manning, 2024) offers a detailed
walkthrough of building a transformer model from scratch, providing insights into the architecture and
implementation details.
Reinforcement Learning
In the supervised learning techniques we covered earlier in this book, the model learns from a specified set of
training data. In reinforcement learning, the model learns as it proceeds, on the basis of feedback from its actions.
The model is given a goal, takes actions, and receives feedback in the form of rewards or penalties. The model
learns to take actions that maximize the cumulative reward over time. AlphaGo and AlphaZero, both developed by
DeepMind (now Google DeepMind), are famous examples of reinforcement learning models that have achieved
superhuman performance in the games of Go and chess, respectively. They were landmark examples of
reinforcement learning because the systems improved by playing millions of games against themselves, optimizing
a reward signal (win/loss) without relying on user-designed strategies.
KEY TERMS FOR REINFORCEMENT LEARNING
Reward
Feedback that an automated agent receives from the environment after taking an action; penalty is a
negative reward
Reinforcement learning
A type of machine learning where an agent learns to make decisions by taking actions in an environment to
maximize cumulative rewards
Agent
The learner or decision-maker algorithm that interacts with the environment and learns from the feedback
it receives
Multi-arm bandits
A continuous experiment in which an agent must choose between multiple options (arms) with unknown
rewards, balancing information-gathering (exploration) and exploitation to maximize rewards
Synonym
bandits
Markov decision processes
A framework for modeling and optimizing decision making, taking account of states, actions, rewards, and
policies
Deep reinforcement learning
Reinforcement learning in which deep neural networks are used to approximate value functions or policies,
enabling the handling of complex, high-dimensional environments
Multi-Arm Bandits
Multi-arm bandits (or, simply, bandits) were an early implementation of reinforcement learning. The name derives
from a gambler playing multiple slot machines, also called “one-armed bandits,” each with a different payout rate.
The gambler’s process is to try out the different machines (arms) multiple times with the goal of figuring out
which has the highest payout rate. The gambler faces a trade-off between exploration (trying out different
machines to gather information about their payout rates) and exploitation (using the machine that has the highest
known payout rate to maximize rewards). This is a more efficient way of exploring different treatments (a
treatment is equivalent to a machine arm) than the classic statistical tests we discussed in Chapter 3. In those
traditional tests, the different treatments would be applied to a set number of subjects, and then, at the end of the
trial period, the results would be assessed. There is no opportunity to adapt to the information gained along the
way.
Agents (the automated players) deploy bandit algorithms to use the intermediate information to adjust the
probabilities of selecting each machine arm. Bandits use different user-specified strategies to balance exploration
with exploitation as they try to optimize the reward signal. For example, the Epsilon first strategy explores
randomly for a set period of time, then exploits the arm (treatment) with the highest payout rate for the rest of the
time. The Epsilon greedy strategy explores randomly with a small probability (epsilon) at each step, while
exploiting the best-known arm most of the time. The Upper Confidence Bound strategy selects arms based on both
their estimated payout rates and the uncertainty around those estimates, favoring arms that have been explored
less.
Sometimes the reward will change depending on context. For example, in music streaming, the reward (say a like
or a download) may depend on the time of day (waking up, exercising, etc.). Contextual bandits take this context
into account when selecting arms, allowing for more personalized decision making.
Multi-arm bandits are popular with digital marketers, who use them to optimize the presentation of online ads and
product recommendations. Related, more constrained versions are used in clinical trial design, where the goal is to
identify both effective and ineffective treatments as fast as possible while maintaining statistical validity. Bandits
and contextual bandits are implemented in the Python package contextualbandits.
Markov Decision Processes
Multi-arm bandits are a special case of Markov decision processes (MDPs), named after the Russian
mathematician Andrey Markov. In an MDP, the algorithm learns a longer-term relationship between decisions and
outcomes, focusing on states and rewards. In chess, for example, the state at any point of play is the configuration
of pieces on the board, and the reward is winning or losing the overall game. States should include relevant history.
For example, in optimizing music streaming recommendations, the state would include prior user activity (clicks,
plays, shares), as well as demographics and other static features. The agent’s actions affect both the next state and
the ultimate reward. The goal of the agent is to learn a policy that maps states to actions in a way that maximizes
the expected cumulative reward over time.
MDPs are sequential processes, where each step in the sequence is defined by the current state, the action taken,
and the instantaneous reward. The agent’s goal is to maximize expected return over time, which is the sum of
discounted instantaneous rewards. The discount factor (gamma) determines the importance of future rewards
compared to immediate rewards.
Consider the following simple inventory illustration (presented by Phil Winder in Reinforcement Learning,
O’Reilly, 2021). A shop has a maximum inventory of 2 units. Each day, the shop can order 0, 1, or 2 units of
inventory. Sales and restocking may happen on a daily basis, with the shop either selling 1 unit (probability = 0.7)
or 0 units (probability = 0.3). Maximum stock is 2, so no restocking takes place if the shop already has 2 units. If a
sale is made, the shop receives a reward of 1. If no sale is made, the reward is 0.
Daily activity can be represented by a transition table (see Table 9-2). Each row shows a possible initial state, an
action, the next state, the probability of that transition, and the immediate reward.
Table 9-2. A transition table for the inventory example
State State P(State |State ,Action)
t Action t+1 t+1 t Reward
0 None 0 0.3 0
0 None 0 0.7 0
0 Restock 1 0.3 0
0 Restock 0 0.7 1
1 None 1 0.3 0
1 None 0 0.7 1
1 Restock 2 0.3 0
1 Restock 1 0.7 1
2 None 2 0.3 0
2 None 1 0.7 1
2 Restock 2 0.7 1
For example, row 3 shows one of the possible transitions: from an initial state of 0 stock, a decision to restock, a
next state of 1, a probability of 0.3 for this transition, and a reward of 0 (since no sales were made). Row 4 covers
the case where a sale happened, starting from the same initial state of 0 stock. Due to restock (+1) and sale (–1),
the new state is still 0. The remaining columns are: a probability of 0.7 for this transition, and a reward of 1 due to
the sale.
This same information can be shown in a transition matrix, shown in Table 9-3 and Table 9-4, less easy to visualize
but closer to the form used in the algorithm. These illustrate transition probabilities from s (row headers) to s’
(column headers).
Table 9-3. No restock transition matrix P(s'|s)
s → s’ 0 1 2
0 1 0 0
1 0.7 0.3 0
2 0 0.7 0.3
Table 9-4. Restock transition matrix P(s'|s)
s → s’ 0 1 2
0 0.7 0.3 0
1 0 0.7 0.3
2 0 0 1
From this information (initial state, action, transition probabilities, reward), MDPs define a value function
measuring the expected value of each state, which is the expected cumulative reward starting from that state and
following the optimal policy. In this simple example, the optimal policy can be determined by iterating through the
transition table and evaluating candidate restocking policies using the transition table until the expected cumulative
reward is maximized.
The optimal value function is expressed by the Bellman equation for the value of a state under an optimal policy:
V (s)=max over [all possible actions] of E[r+γ×V (s′)]
* *
where V * (s) is the optimal value of state s, r is the immediate reward, γ is the discount factor, and s′ is the next
state resulting from taking an action in state s. Although it appears to account only for the current state and the
next state, note that V * (s′) is determined recursively, i.e., it necessarily incorporates one step beyond and thus
accounts for the entire future sequence of states and rewards. Typically, rewards in the future sequence are
discounted, so, although the sequence may extend indefinitely, the value of the rewards diminishes to a point
where the sequence converges to a finite value.
Deep Reinforcement Learning
In the numerical example discussed in the preceding section, the states, actions, rewards, and transition
probabilities are simple, known, and easy to visualize. In more complex real-world problems, the state space can
be very large or continuous, the transition probabilities may be unknown, and the reward structure may be
complex. This is where deep learning comes in, as it can be used to approximate the value function without
needing to explicitly model or simulate all the possible states and transitions.
In deep reinforcement learning, the value function (or sometimes the policy) is represented by a neural network
that takes the current state as input and produces as output either an action or a probability distribution over
possible actions. After interacting with the environment, the network’s weights are adjusted so that actions leading
to higher long-term reward become more likely, while less successful actions become less likely. Through repeated
experience and incremental weight updates, the network gradually improves its behavior toward maximizing
expected discounted cumulative reward.
In reinforcement learning, data is sequential in nature. More specifically, reinforcement learning operates on
trajectories: state → action → next state → reward, repeated over time. Each action reshapes the distribution of
future inputs. Deep models such as RNNs or transformers can incorporate temporal context directly, allowing the
policy to condition on prior observations when the environment does not fully reveal its state. Thus, optimization
occurs over sequences of interactions rather than isolated examples.
Here it is useful to highlight a key distinction between reinforcement learning and supervised learning. Unlike in
supervised learning, where the data distribution is external to the model, in reinforcement learning the agent’s
policy determines which states it will encounter in the future. Thus, learning and data collection are intertwined: as
the policy changes, so does the training distribution.
KEY IDEAS
Reinforcement learning (RL) is different from the statistical and machine learning methods discussed
to this point in that RL involves interaction between the learner and the environment.
Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), where the environment consists of human
preferences, is used in the context of large language models. In this process, humans rank different
model responses, and those rankings are used to train a reward model.
RL receives rewards from the environment and uses this information to make better decisions.
Multi-arm bandits and Markov decision processes are constrained frameworks for modeling RL
problems.
Deep RL uses deep neural networks to approximate value functions or policies, yielding more
powerful models that can handle high dimensionality and more complex environments.
Further Reading
Bandit Algorithms for Website Optimization by John Myles White (O’Reilly, 2012) is a short, highly
accessible treatment of multi-arm bandits.
Reinforcement Learning by Phil Winder (O’Reilly, 2021) provides a comprehensive introduction to
reinforcement learning, covering both foundational concepts and practical applications, with a focus on
deep reinforcement learning.
Summary
This chapter gave an overview of deep learning and its evolution from RNNs to CNNs to transformer
architectures. We discussed how convolutional neural networks (CNNs) work by learning hierarchical features
from data, particularly in image recognition tasks. We provided an example of how CNNs can be used for image
classification and how transfer learning allows practitioners to leverage pretrained models for specific tasks. We
then introduced transformers, which rely on attention mechanisms to capture relationships in data, and discussed
their architecture and key components. We touched on the importance of large data sets and powerful computing
resources in enabling the training of modern transformer models. Finally, we reviewed reinforcement learning, in
which algorithms interact with the environment and, via rewards and penalties, optimize decision policies. In the
next chapter, we explore how these technologies evolve beyond prediction and decision optimization and become
tools for generating text and images.
Exploration with AI
Ask your AI for a Python or R example of an LSTM network for time-series forecasting. How does it
compare to a random forest or boosted trees on the same data?
Ask your AI to translate the CNN example in this chapter to R or using a different framework in Python
(e.g., tensorflow).
In this chapter, we covered the decoder architecture for transformers (e.g., GPT). Ask your AI to explain
the differences between encoder-only, decoder-only, and encoder-decoder architectures, and provide
examples of tasks for which each architecture is best suited.
Ask your AI to produce a simple example of how the attention mechanism works in a transformer model,
using a small sentence and showing the calculations for the query, key, and value vectors, as well as the
resulting attention weights.
We didn’t provide a detailed explanation of how the softmax temperature parameter works in transformer
models. Ask your AI to explain how the temperature parameter modifies the softmax function and how it
influences the sampling process during text generation.
Ask your AI to produce code for simple machine translation from one language to another using a
transformer model, and see how the attention mechanism helps the model to align words in the source
language with words in the target language during translation.
Ask your AI to explain the different strategies for balancing exploration and exploitation in multi-arm
bandits, and to review their comparative strengths and weaknesses.
In both online marketing and in drug development, the goal is to select the best-performing “treatment”
(e.g., ad, drug) as quickly as possible. Ask your AI to explain the important differences between these two
domains, and how these differences affect the choice of algorithm for optimizing decisions. (Hint:
regulatory considerations are paramount in drug development. See if your AI highlights this in its
response. If it fails to stress this angle and to explain the implications, ask it to do so.)
1
“A Fast Learning Algorithm for Deep Belief Nets” with Osindero and Teh, and “Reducing the Dimensionality of Data with Neural Networks”
with Salakhutdinov.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 10. Generative AI and
Large Language Models
In this chapter,1 we take machine learning well beyond inference and
prediction, and into the realm of creating. Responding to instructions,
generative AI algorithms can create new verbal content (written or spoken)
and produce new images, videos, and audio (including music).
No machine learning technology has generated as much popular fanfare as
generative AI. Stories about it appear on a daily basis in the business and
popular press. Generative AI’s popular profile was enhanced by the public
availability of powerful generative AI tools such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT,
with which anyone can type in a prompt and generate impressive results.
THE TURING TEST
Alan Turing was a British mathematician and computer science pioneer
who, among many other achievements, played a key role in breaking
German ciphers used during WWII. This work contributed to the Allied
victory in the Battle of the Atlantic. The Turing test, proposed by Alan
Turing in 1950, is a benchmark for evaluating a computer’s
communication ability. In the test, a human evaluator interacts with
both a human and a machine through written communication, without
knowing which is which. If the evaluator cannot reliably tell the
machine from the human, the machine is said to have passed the test.
Generative AI models are generally agreed to be the first computer
technology to have passed the Turing test. In fact, in a randomized
experiment in 2025, ChatGPT-4.5 was identified as human more than twice
as often as the actual human subject.
Generative AI’s capabilities have made it a powerful tool for businesses and
other organizations.
Generative AI and LLMs
Generative AI is a class of models that have the ability to produce text,
images, audio, or video on the basis of a user-supplied prompt (a written
query, request, or instruction). It is an extension of supervised learning and
of the neural nets used in deep learning (see Chapter 8 and Chapter 9).
KEY TERMS FOR GENERATIVE AI
Large language model
A machine learning model that can generate lengthy text output in
report or narrative form
Synonym
LLM
Temperature
A setting that controls the randomness (creativity) of the output of a
generative AI model
Synonym
creativity setting
Models that produce written text, such as OpenAI’s ChatGPT and Google’s
Gemini, are called large language models (LLMs). The providers of public
versions of these models say they were trained on a wide variety and huge
volume of written content available online, including websites (Wikipedia
and many others), books, news articles, and more. However, the companies
do not disclose specifics about what has been used in training (for example,
they are cagey about their use of social media).
Predicting the Next Word
LLMs have at their heart the supervised learning task of predicting the next
word, given a prompt. In itself, this is an old idea—it has stood behind
autocomplete features for decades. Autocomplete algorithms answer the
question “Given a user-supplied set of words or letters, what words or
letters are likely to come next?” What is new about LLMs, compared to
earlier autocomplete algorithms, is the expanded use of context: each
generated next word is based not just on the immediate prompt but also on
the lengthy (sometimes a page or more) sequence of words that the LLM
has already generated in response to the prompt. Second- and later-
generation LLMs also take into account additional factors:
An optional user-assigned role (for example, you are a university
professor, or you are a thriller writer). This influences the style
and content of the response.
The prior exchanges in the conversation between the LLM and the
user. This keeps the conversation coherent and enriches the
content.
Prior conversations (perhaps spanning sessions) between the LLM
and the user. This helps the user who is engaged in a long-term
project.
External sources of information, such as a knowledge base or the
internet. This helps keep the LLM’s responses current and
accurate.
The ability to write and execute code. This helps the LLM to return
accurate results for numerical and data-related queries.
LLM-generated sequences must not only answer the user’s query but also
follow the rules of grammar and syntax. They can follow a style (outline,
summary, list, table, literary genre) dictated by the prompt.
Creativity and Temperature
Typically, the default settings for an LLM call for it to produce the most
likely next word, given the prompts and context. However, there are usually
multiple possible completion words, with different probabilities attached to
each. In some situations, it may be useful to instruct the model not to
always select the most probable word. For example, if you are writing
advertising copy and want to avoid repetition, you may want the LLM to be
more creative by sometimes choosing less likely words. This is done by
adjusting a parameter called temperature. A low temperature (close to zero)
means the model will almost always choose the most likely next word. A
high temperature (say, above 1.0) means the model will choose less likely
words more often, resulting in more diverse and creative outputs. A
temperature of 1 means the model will choose the next word with
probability proportional to its estimated likelihood. A higher temperature
decreases that probability (i.e., it increases randomness), while a lower
temperature makes the output more deterministic, and less likely to “make
things up” or hallucinate. Hallucinations are a recognized problem with
LLMs, and a primary reason for keeping a human in the loop while using
them (see the section “Alignment” later in this chapter).
Temperature can be set via API calls. For example, in OpenAI’s API, you
can set the temperature parameter in your request body like this:
{
"model": "gpt-4",
"prompt": "Write a poem about the ocean",
"temperature": 0.9
}
Effects similar to temperature changes can be achieved by the nature of the
prompt and its effect on the distribution of possible next words, rather than
on the mechanism of selecting the next word. A prompt that asks for a poem
would automatically generate a creative response (via a broadening of the
distribution of possible next words), while a prompt seeking a summary of a
financial report to regulators would (one hopes) need less creativity (a
narrower distribution of possible next words).
KEY IDEAS
Generative AI is able to produce lengthy text as well as images
in response to a prompt.
It is an extension of the text-mining capability of predicting the
next word.
The creativity of the output can be controlled by a temperature
setting.
Learning Language and Generating Images:
Attention
How do LLMs learn language and master content? We have already
introduced one of the key building blocks in Chapter 9: deep learning. That
building block was the concept of learning higher-level features. We
discussed it in the context of “understanding” images by moving from
individual pixel values to representing local features like edges, then
higher-level features like shapes, and even higher-level features like faces.
We applied neural networks to text and introduced the concept of word
embedding, in which each token (word or term) is represented by a vector
encoding its numerical scores on numerous dimensions, which the network
has learned are useful representations of meaning. We also saw how these
vectors, representing individual words, could then be used in both
predictive algorithms and unsupervised learning.
KEY TERMS FOR LEARNING LANGUAGE
Attention
A process for incorporating context from distant words in a text
sequence
Token-pair
A pair of tokens (words) from a document, typically one of all such
possible pairs
Context window
A subset of a document used to compute attention scores
Text mining deep learning networks, including the RNN and LSTM
networks that were introduced in Chapter 9, focus on neighboring words to
contribute context that improves predictive performance.
Generative AI requires moving beyond the context of the immediate
neighborhood. If you consider that the output of a generative AI model may
constitute extensive narrative text and require command of the rules and
structure of a language, you can see that the algorithm must take into
account words that might be quite distant from one another. To be effective
and computationally feasible, the model must still have a way of focusing
on relevant content, whether it’s adjacent or distant. The mechanism for
doing this is called attention. With the word embedding vectors as building
blocks, the algorithm goes through a series of matrix operations to compute
attention scores for each token-pair within a context window (subset of the
corpus) whose size is determined by the model and its architecture. Each
token then ends up with a set of scores that reflect the relevance of each
other token in the data. These attention scores are converted to weights and
used to revise the original word embedding in a way that gives greater
weight to more relevant information, no matter where it may be located in
the window. The computation of attention scores happens both in training,
when weights are updated, and in inference (use of the model, no weight
updating).
The need to compute a score for each token-pair makes model training and
inference extremely computationally intensive. A modification termed
sparse attention reduces the number of token-pairs that need to be scored,
reducing this computational burden. There are several ways to reduce
computation. For example, for a given token, tokens that are nearby might
all be paired up and scored, while for more distant tokens, only every n-th
token is paired. Another approach is to use a sliding window, where only
tokens within a certain distance are paired. In late 2025, the Chinese maker
of DeepSeek sought market advantage by reducing the cost of API calls to
its LLM by 50%, which it claimed it was able to do by using sparse
attention.
KEY IDEAS
Attention is the process by which the LLM, in predicting the
next token, incorporates context from a broad context window,
not just from nearby tokens.
The LLM does this using attention scores for each token-pair,
reflecting their relevance to each other.
Further Reading
“Attention is All You Need” by Vaswani et al., 2017, is the seminal paper
introducing the attention mechanism that underpins most LLMs.
Reinforcement Learning from Human
Feedback
As we saw earlier in this book, the foundation of supervised machine
learning is training a model to match its predictions to known outcomes. In
generative AI, the outcome is not a “known” outcome but rather a word
sequence that is useful to a human user.
KEY TERMS FOR REINFORCEMENT LEARNING FROM
HUMAN FEEDBACK
Reinforcement learning from human feedback
The incorporation of human feedback as rewards to improve LLM
performance
Synonym
RLHF
Alignment learning
The general process of making model output consistent with some
benchmark
Reward
A score given to model output that reflects human preferences
Reward model
A model trained to predict and economize on scarce human
feedback
Direct preference optimization
Like a reward model, except it’s folded into the main model, not
kept separate
Synonym
DPO
Reinforcement learning from AI feedback
The use of AI-generated feedback as rewards to improve LLM
performance
Synonym
RLAIF
The process of making model output consistent with a benchmark is termed
alignment learning. A frequent benchmark is human preferences, and for
this, human rating of model outputs is needed. The core of this rating is
human review (often paid) of model output. Early LLMs were trained with
prompts matched to human-supplied answers; the LLM was trained to try to
match the answer. This is an example of reinforcement learning from human
feedback (RLHF), in which models interact with reward functions to
optimize actions and decisions. AlphaZero’s mastery of chess, where
winning is the reward, is a classic example of reinforcement learning. In
RLHF the key ingredient in the reward function is human feedback.
Alignment
Reinforcement learning with human feedback is an important tool in the
process of alignment—making model outputs more in line with human
needs. This means making them more helpful, more truthful, and safer.
Safety is partly a subjective concept, but it might include things like
avoiding telling users how to construct a bomb. These key elements of
alignment are often summarized as helpful, honest, and harmless, or HHH.
There is a distinction to be made between human needs as expressed by
users, as with RLHF, and human needs as specified by model developers, as
is sometimes done to make models more ethical. The latter are sometimes
hard-coded, as with Google Photos’ elimination of gorilla, chimpanzee, and
monkey as potential labels, because the photo recognition algorithm was
labeling dark-skinned individuals with these labels.
Reward Models
Human ratings are expensive and, in machine learning terms, scarce.
During training, an LLM might make hundreds of passes through its
network (which has hundreds of billions of weights or parameters) per
second. Clearly it cannot stop for human input on the prediction each time!
Therefore, an artificial method of awarding scores to output was developed
to supplement the human ratings. This method is called a reward model.
The reward model (separate from the main model) is trained on human
ratings to predict human ratings on future LLM output. This process
produces scores to be applied to LLM outputs during the LLM’s training.
The human ratings used for training the reward model are relatively sparse
—the reward model might yield tens of thousands of scores for every
human score.
Reward models also incorporated rating systems that encouraged more
creativity by the LLM, with raters giving model outputs a rank score, or a
choice between two responses. A model could also be evaluated using
certain triggers in the conversation, such as “I did not understand what you
said about… .” These devices can all be incorporated into the normal flow
of LLM use without requiring dedicated paid reviewers, substantially
increasing the quantity of real human feedback.
Later processes such as direct preference optimization (DPO) bypassed the
separate training of a reward function: when the main model itself is
trained, the loss function is trained so that it rewards model outputs in
accord with human preferences.
Still later developments include reinforcement learning from AI feedback
(RLAIF), in which rewards are based on feedback from another AI model
rather than from humans. RLAIF is another way to economize on scarce
human feedback, which is reserved for upstream tasks—the establishment
of the rules by which RLAIF is to operate, and, in some cases, the fine-
tuning of the AI model providing feedback. It also has the advantage of
speed (LLMs are faster than humans) and structural scalability (the process,
once established and tuned, can readily be adapted across tasks). The
absence of human feedback in RLAIF cuts two ways: on the one hand,
human preferences and values are lost; on the other hand, a consistent set of
values and governing preferences may be enforced.
The difference between RLAIF on the one hand, and RLHF, reward model
training, and DPO on the other, is mainly the scale and position of the
human feedback. In contrast to the latter methods, RLAIF uses human
feedback only to set and tune the rules and parameters for the AI feedback
model, and is not part of the main training process.
KEY IDEAS
Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) uses
human preferences as rewards to guide LLM learning.
These human preferences, which are scarce, are augmented by
predicted preference scores.
Reinforcement learning from AI feedback (RLAIF) substitutes
AI feedback for human feedback in the training process.
Further Reading
Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction by Richard S. Sutton and Andrew
G. Barto, Bradford Books (first edition 1998, second edition 2018)
Image Generation
Perhaps no AI capability captures the popular imagination more than image
and video creation. Most LLMs can now generate images and video as well
as text. The company TikTok has built its entire business model around
short videos, many of which are now generated by AI. In promoting his
own AI model, Grok, on his social media platform, X, Elon Musk tweets
mostly about its video creation ability. It is notable that deep learning also
saw its initial successes in the fields of natural language processing and
computer vision.
KEY TERMS FOR IMAGE GENERATION
Diffusion methods
Models that learn to iteratively denoise a randomly perturbed
image, gradually refining it into a realistic output
Reference image
A real image (i.e., nonrandom) to which the model iteratively
compares its generated image during training
Seed image
An image, typically of randomly generated pixels, that starts the
process of image generation
Denoising
Modifying a generated image to bring it closer to the actual image
Embedded text vector
A numerical vector representation of text that captures its higher-
level features
Conditioning
The process of incorporating text input into the image generation
process
Models that produce images on the basis of prompts combine text
processing capabilities with image generation. As with LLMs, the key lies
in higher-level feature learning. Recall from Chapter 9 that higher-level
features in images are represented by numerical vectors. The same is true
for text, as we saw in our discussion of text mining. So both images and text
can be viewed by AI through the same lens: numerical vectors that embody
meaning. The challenge in image generation is to bring these two different
modalities together so that the model can generate images that correspond
to text descriptions. A popular architecture for image generation, diffusion
methods, works roughly as follows:
1. A set of real images with text descriptions is assembled as a
reference base.
2. The text descriptions are converted to embedded text vectors (see
Chapter 9).
3. A random noise seed image is created by generating random pixel
values.
4. Iteratively and gradually, the random image is denoised by
repeatedly modifying it in a way that brings it closer to the real
image while incorporating the embedded text description in the
process, an approach called conditioning.
5. In the training phase, a loss function guides the training process by
comparing the denoised image to the reference image.
6. Once the model is trained, it works without reference images,
simply generating images that are close to the text descriptions.
Integrating Text Input
A key component of this process is incorporating the user’s text input. In
our discussion of text mining (see Chapter 9), we saw that text embedding
converts text to a numerical vector that represents the latent high-level
features in the text, analogous to the autoencoder in Figure 10-1.
Figure 10-1. Derivation of higher-level features in autoencoder
The higher-level features for both the image and the text prompt are now
represented by numerical vectors, and, in effect, can talk the same language.
The use of these high-level representations to match images to appropriate
text descriptions is a form of alignment learning. Of course, these layered
procedures yield an overall process that is extremely computationally
intensive—it is easy to see how text and image generation are driving
extraordinary demand for computing resources. At the time of this writing,
diffusion methods are the dominant image generation method. Earlier
related architectures include generative adversarial networks or GANs, and
variational autoencoders. GANs also work with initial images that are
random noise, but they have a separate component called a discriminator
whose explicit function is to identify whether an image is real or fake.
Variational autoencoders generate the initial seed images (the noisy image
that gets turned into a realistic image) by sampling and generating from the
latent high-level feature components in the center of Figure 10-1 rather than
by simply generating random pixel values.
KEY IDEAS
Models are first trained to iteratively modify random pixels to
match a reference image.
Then, without reference images, they generate images to match
a text description.
The key is that both images and text are represented by higher-
level feature vectors that “speak the same language.”
Further Reading
“Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models” by Ho et al., 2020
Scale and Power
Generative AI models require massive computing power for both training
and use, and at a scale well beyond anything needed by traditional statistical
and machine learning models working with structured data. The latter might
need seconds or minutes of computing time on a single computer. In
contrast, training an LLM such as GPT-4 might require thousands of
computers working in parallel for weeks on end.
KEY TERMS FOR SCALE AND POWER
Parallelization
The processing of instructions concurrently in parallel, rather than
sequentially
Graphics processing unit
A computer chip designed for parallel processing, originally for
graphics applications but now widely used in machine learning
Synonym
GPU
Mixture of experts
A large model with many submodels that specialize in handling
certain types of input
Synonym
MoE
Emergent capabilities
Qualitatively improved capabilities that seem to appear suddenly as
models continue to scale
Artificial general intelligence
Imagined future AI that has cognitive capabilities indistinguishable
from human intelligence
Synonym
AGI
The electricity required to train a large LLM is equivalent to that used by a
small city. LLM providers are now in the electricity business, in some cases
building their own nuclear power plants to ensure a reliable and cost-
effective supply of electricity.
Parallelization and GPUs
The advent of attention techniques is one source of the scaling
requirements. The process of calculating and weighting attention scores
calls for more computation than the sequential processes of RNNs and
requires parallelization of computing to be efficient. For parallelization to
really yield speed advances, computer chips must take advantage of parallel
processing. By happenstance, chips particularly suited to parallel processing
had already been developed some time prior to the advent of generative AI.
Relatively expensive, these chips, GPUs, were produced to support the
intensive graphical operations in computer games and 3D visualization
applications. NVIDIA, the primary maker of such chips, was a primary
beneficiary when GPUs were repurposed for the generative AI boom.
As parameters (weights) are added to neural nets, performance improves.
They now number in the trillions (see Figure 10-2). LLMs have graduated
from “autocomplete on steroids” to full command of language and the
ability to both follow complex instructions and generate complex content.
As more parameters are added incrementally, performance improves
incrementally. Results appear faster and they are more complete.
Figure 10-2. Size of LLMs over time (GPT-3.5 to GPT-5 values are estimates)
Mixture of Experts Models
As LLM architecture evolves, not all tasks require the use of all parameters.
Mixture of experts (MoE) models route different tasks to different
submodels, each with a subset of the overall parameters. This reduces the
computational burden for any given task, while retaining the overall size
and power of the full model. How does this work?
When the model is designed, a gating layer network assesses the prompt
and routes it to one of the model’s expert subnetworks. Starting out, those
subnetworks and the gating network are initialized randomly, but during
training they learn that learning can be optimized by specialization—some
subnetworks become experts in certain types of tasks. The designer
specifies only the number of experts, and the overall system determines
what they specialize in. These specialties may be interpretable (e.g., one
expert for math problems, another for literary analysis) or not. During
training, only the parameters in the selected expert subnetwork are updated,
reducing the computational burden. During inference (actual use of the
model), only the selected expert is used, again reducing computation.
Note the similarity to the role of different heads in multi-head attention
architecture, where different heads focus on learning different patterns (see
“Architecture Overview” in Chapter 9). The role of different convolutional
filters to identify different features in CNN architecture is also analogous
(see “CNNs” in Chapter 9).
Emergent Capabilities
Many computer scientists believe that as models scale, performance can
also increase not just incrementally but also qualitatively in leaps, with new
capabilities emerging once a certain threshold is crossed. This can
encompass new and different abilities, such as the ability to do arithmetic.
Early LLMs were remarkably poor at arithmetic, but, at some point,
apparently around the 100 billion-parameter mark, LLMs began to do
arithmetic better.
RICKY RICARDO AND LITTLE RED RIDING HOOD
To better understand the concept of emergent capabilities, consider a
simpler analogy from decades ago, illustrating the advance of AI via
more data (rather than more complex models). In the early days of the
web, searches on the phrase Ricky Ricardo Little Red Riding Hood
would have yielded various links to the I Love Lucy TV show, other
links to Ricardo’s career as a band leader, and links to the children’s
story of Little Red Riding Hood. Searches on those simple components
of the phrase were common, even in the internet’s early days. However,
they were not that specific. Once the search engine had gathered
sufficient data on searches and clicks, the comparatively rare searches
for the entire phrase would begin to appear and then accumulate
sufficiently to point the search engine to the exact I Love Lucy episode
in which Ricky enacts, in a comic mixture of Spanish and English,
Little Red Riding Hood for his infant son. This result would be exactly
what the user was looking for. The more general results from older
search models (to the television show, the band, or the children’s story)
would be close to useless by comparison. The emergence of this
capability might have appeared to be relatively sudden—it would
follow a considerable period of growth in data and model maturity
during which the long-tail data needed for a successful search had
simply not yet accumulated.
Artificial General Intelligence
Some of the excitement around generative AI stems from speculation that it
may soon lead to artificial general intelligence (AGI)—capabilities similar
to human intelligence. In the words of ChatGPT, this means “a broad
spectrum of cognitive abilities, enabling it [AGI] to adapt and excel in
various situations without needing to be explicitly programmed for each
task.” For some observers, the concept of emergent capabilities is connected
with the fear that AI will develop to the point where it threatens humans.
Indeed, OpenAI sees its mission as “building safe and beneficial AGI.” AGI
is still in the future, and its exact nature may be hard to pin down when it
arrives. For now, it is difficult to imagine it being disconnected from
human-assigned tasks, though those tasks may increasingly be higher-level
goals.
Whether LLMs can develop new capabilities as they scale is still a matter of
debate, and depends partly on how you define “develop new capabilities.”
At this writing, examples of LLMs undertaking independent activity that is
not in response to a task set by humans and that might pose a societal risk
fall in the realm of hypothetical scenarios.
KEY IDEAS
LLMs require huge scale (number of parameters, size of input
data, number of computations, use of electricity).
To facilitate computation, they need parallelization and
specialized GPU chips.
Mixture of experts models improve efficiency by routing tasks
to specialized submodels.
Qualitatively improved abilities appear to emerge with scale.
Further Reading
Programming Massively Parallel Processors: A Hands-on
Approach, 4th ed., by Wen-mei W. Hwu, David B. Kirk, and Izzat
El Hajj (Morgan Kaufmann, 2022); Kirk was one of the original
developers of GPU architecture at NVIDIA.
“Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models,” by Jason Wei et
al., 2022, origin of the term emergent capabilities.
Training LLMs and Adapting LLMs for
Specific Purposes
Training of generic LLMs (foundation models), such as GPT, requires
thousands of computers operating for weeks on end, and uses electricity
equivalent to that used by a small city. This does the heavy lifting of
learning language: grammar, vocabulary, syntax, and style. These pretrained
models have learned language amazingly well and are suitable for general
functions that rely on knowledge of the internet, such as improving web
search functionality.
KEY TERMS FOR TRAINING LLMS
Foundation model
Primary LLM fully trained with language or image capabilities, not
targeted at a specific purpose
Synonyms
base model, pretrained model, generic model, public LLM
Knowledge cutoff
The date up to which a foundation LLM’s learning is current
Fine-tuning
The process of further training a foundation model on data from a
specific domain
Retrieval-augmented generation
The process of directing a foundation LLM to reference a specific
data source in generating responses
Synonym
RAG
Production LLM
An LLM deployed in a pipeline to ingest prompts and produce
responses automatically
Synonym
production model
Prompt
A user instruction or query to an LLM
Prompt engineering
The process of designing effective prompts (particularly automated
prompts) to elicit useful responses from an LLM, particularly a
production LLM
For more focused purposes tied to a specific domain, additional techniques
are often needed. Often, the data required for training is proprietary and not
available to a public LLM. For example, if a drug distributor needs a report
on prescription patterns and anomalies, GPT could not do the job—the
public internet does not have the relevant data. There is also the problem
that the foundation model incorporates knowledge only up to the date of its
training, the knowledge cutoff issue.
Fine-Tuning
One approach is to take a pretrained model such as GPT and train it further
on data specific to the domain of interest. This is called fine-tuning. The
drug distributor might feed the model its own records on shipments and
prescriptions for the drugs that it carries, then submit queries. Often, the
task-specific data might be labeled, and supervised learning is a component
of the overall process. For example, a call center might have records of
incoming queries and actual human responses. The general LLM can be
fine-tuned by training on these query–response pairs, and learn to generate
appropriate responses to new queries.
To make the fine-tuning process more efficient, the base model weights
may be frozen and a smaller set of parameters trained on the task-specific
data and applied at inference with the base model. One such approach is
low-rank adaptation (LoRA), in which a low-dimensional subspace is
learned from task-specific training data and is then applied, along with the
full model, on the same or closely related tasks.
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)
Another approach is to specify a reference set of records that are relevant to
a query, then apply an LLM to them. This approach, termed retrieval-
augmented generation (RAG) works roughly as follows:
The query is embedded in a numeric vector (see Chapter 9).
The user specifies a set of records deemed relevant to the problem,
or uses information retrieval methods (such as those described in
Chapter 9) to retrieve records from the reference records that are
close to (similar to) the query vector.
The LLM is used to process and refine the specified or retrieved
records and, taking advantage of its command of human language,
produce a logically flowing summary or report that responds to the
query.
In the RAG approach, the LLM is not actually trained on the records of
interest. Its role is to craft a good response based on the retrieved records.
RAG has advantages when the reference records change frequently, and
being current is important. It does well when the knowledge required of the
reference data is limited enough that the relevant records yielded by the
comparatively simple nearness queries are indeed sufficient to provide a
good response. RAG does not change the LLM itself. In cases where a deep
and detailed knowledge of the reference data is needed (medical diagnosis,
for example), fine-tuning of the LLM on that data may perform better than
RAG. In this case, the LLM itself is modified.
Web Searches
Another approach is for the LLM to search the web for current information
that is relevant to the prompt. This method is used by default when the AI
being used is integrated into a standard web search. In other cases, the
LLM’s reference to the web may be triggered by characteristics of the
prompt. Reliance on web searches can improve the currency and accuracy
of the LLM’s responses, but it also introduces the risk that the LLM will
retrieve and incorporate inaccurate or misleading information from the web.
Therefore, human review of LLM outputs that rely on web searches is
especially important.
Prompt Engineering
The efficiency and power of the LLM for a specific purpose is affected by
the quality of the prompts. What are the characteristics of good prompts?
It is useful to distinguish between two scenarios for prompting:
Individual queries on public LLMs, such as ChatGPT
Production use of generative AI in business settings, such as
automated response systems for customer inquiries
Interactive prompting
For individual queries, the prompts for LLMs bring the user into a
conversation with the LLM. The LLM can handle detailed and very specific
queries. The user can read the response, which may be over a page long,
and ask clarifying questions. The LLM will remember its own prior
response in formulating a clarification. If you recall that LLMs have fully
learned human language, then you will realize that the most important
attribute of good prompts is clarity. Avoid ambiguity—the LLM ingests
your query literally and cannot read your mind. You should also be specific
—recall the legal maxim, “The specific trumps the general.”
LLMs respond volubly to open-ended queries (tell me about… ), as these are
a normal characteristic of the language that was used to train the model.
Open-ended prompts are useful in an exploratory phase of an investigation,
but you should be specific (otherwise the LLM will fall back on its well-
learned content that may be true, but not directly relevant). Similarly, LLMs
are adept at tasks that require more than a simple response (please
summarize…) . Follow-up questions are also good (can you explain what
you meant by… ).
Prompt engineering in production use
For individual queries on public LLMs, users can iteratively refine their
prompts on the fly if the initial response is not satisfactory or clear. This is
not true for generative AI deployed in automated business settings. In those
settings, prompts are assembled programmatically repeatedly for a given
task (e.g., responding to a customer inquiry). They are passed to the LLM
automatically in rapid succession, with no opportunity for review or
iteration with a human in the loop. Hence, investment in the process for
generating prompts pays off. This process is called prompt engineering.
Consider the problem of reviewing hundreds of thousands of documents
produced during legal discovery in a litigation. As the documents are fed to
a text-mining model, a single prompt might be applied to each document
along the lines of, “Is this document relevant to the case?” Along with the
document itself, the prompt might include the document’s source, its length
(many documents might have to be truncated), and other attributes. Since
this prompt will be applied many thousands of times in the pipeline, it is
worth investing time in refining the prompt to get the best possible results.
Phoenix and Taylor (2024) identify five principles for effective prompt
engineering (they also apply to interactive prompting):
Give direction
Make sure the prompt is structured to elicit what you need. Using our
illustration of legal discovery, for example, you will want the prompt to
ask whether the document is relevant or not. Other information that you
need should be specified as well.
Specify format
Define the rules for the output format (e.g., a list or a table). For the
legal illustration, it might be “return ‘relevant’ or ‘not relevant’ and, if
relevant, provide a 100-word summary.”
Provide examples
Provide some illustrations of good responses. This approach is called
few-shot learning, and stands in contrast to zero-shot learning, which is
the standard approach of submitting a prompt without clarifying
examples. Few-shot examples are often used to illustrate the desired
format, in which case they need not contain accurate information.
Test and evaluate
Try out different prompts and evaluate the results, identifying
significant errors and what caused them.
Divide labor
Split complex tasks into simpler subtasks, each with its own prompt.
Pattern Matching and In-Context Learning
If you use an LLM in an ongoing project, you may notice that it picks up on
patterns in your prompts, and the project in general. For example, if you are
preparing simultaneously a slide deck, speaker notes, and a parallel written
document, the LLM may learn the structure of these documents and begin
to anticipate your needs. As you write the bullets for the slide, it will
suggest language for the narrative document and the speaker notes. This is
called in-context learning. The LLM is not being retrained; rather, it is
picking up on patterns in your prompts and the documents it has generated
for you. Some LLM tools (e.g., Copilot) incorporate this capability without
requiring you to specifically create prompts. The tool will feed prompts to
the LLM that incorporate both the structure and the content of the work as it
evolves. The LLM then suggests next steps, text blocks, or code snippets
that fit the context of the work.
Interactive Conversation
The ability to carry on a conversation is a key skill in learning a new
language, and, as we noted, out-of-the-box LLMs can do this well enough
at a general level to pass the so-called Turing test. Duolingo, the language
learning app, has tuned its LLM so it supports conversation at a level
appropriate to a specific learner, and also references prior elements in the
conversation. For example, one Duolingo conversation (taken from the
author’s experience) referenced items mentioned in a previous
conversation:
Duolingo: How do you like to decorate your house? Do you like
candles?
Student: No, I like plants.
Duolingo: Can you describe your favorite plant?
Student: It has green leaves and red flowers.
Duolingo: Oh, that must go well with your blue vases.
KEY IDEAS
Foundation models can be adapted for specific domains.
They can be fine-tuned (further trained) with data from the
domain.
Via RAG, they can be directed to a specific data set to draw
knowledge (no further training).
They can be designed to augment their own knowledge with
more current web searches.
For production use, prompt engineering is important.
For individual use, ongoing interactive conversation is
possible.
Further Reading
Prompt Engineering for Generative AI by James Phoenix and Mike
Taylor, O’Reilly, 2024
“Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP
Tasks” by Lewis et al., 2020
Deploying and Using Generative AI
Tech moguls and philosophers speculate about the aspects of generative AI
that seem derived from science fiction, but the most compelling near-term
use cases range from fundamental changes to entire professions to
incremental improvements on existing business processes.
KEY TERMS FOR DEPLOYING AND USING
GENERATIVE AI
Data augmentation
The use of LLMs to create synthetic training data
Data cannibalization
The degradation of model performance due to training on AI-
generated data
Agentic AI
AI being used in pursuit of higher-level goals that require it to
perform subtasks
Chain-of-thought reasoning
Ability of an AI model to manifest intermediate output prior to a
full response
Synonym
CoT
Here are some examples of the practical use cases:
Translation services, both written and spoken, are increasingly
provided by LLMs.
Marketers use it to write first drafts of advertising copy and
promotional materials, and to generate images for ads and social
media posts.
Tech support and customer service operations can use it to respond
more precisely to customer inquiries and improve upon existing
clumsy and tedious systems.
Lawyers use it to research case law (though they must be careful in
relying on its drafting of legal arguments, and always verify what it
produces).
Doctors and therapists use it to summarize patient visits and
generate notes (it needs to be HIPAA-compliant).
Programmers use it to generate code snippets and even entire
programs. Software developers can set out specifications in natural
language and get back code blocks. (This capability is best
supervised by expert coders who can check the code for
correctness and security.) Generative AI has increased the
productivity of coders at all levels and reduced the need for junior-
level software engineering positions.
Data scientists can use it to explore data efficiently, engineer
features, iterate on models, and document their work. The more
specific the task, the better; LLMs struggle with data ambiguity
and messiness.
Producers of video games use a combination of reinforcement
learning and generative AI to create personalized content (enemies,
or quests, for example) for individuals based on a player’s
attributes and play. The same process can be used with digital
casino games to personalize gamblers’ experiences and foster
greater use (thereby enhancing the addiction of compulsive
gamblers).
Some of these uses suggest that the future value in AI may lie less in
generic LLMs, which are being commoditized, but rather in LLMs tuned
for specific purposes and, especially in proprietary data that underlies such
uses. LexisNexis, the online legal documentation source, now offers
generative AI along with its traditional legal research option. According to
its website, customers can get “conversational search, drafting,
summarization, document analysis, and… linked legal citations.” The huge
data requirements of generative AI strengthen the competitive position of
LexisNexis, which has enormous proprietary legal content at its disposal
(790 times the volume of Wikipedia, it says).
Augmenting AI Training Data
One of the hallmarks of generative AI is the need for huge amounts of data
to create models. In many cases, available data may be inadequate and
machine learning algorithms may benefit from additional data. Consider
brain scans and efforts to use them in understanding brain disease. MRIs are
costly, and brain disorders are quite complex. Existing brain scans may not
provide enough ammunition for ML algorithms to fully “understand” this
complexity in some cases.
Generative AI can be used to augment training data in such cases,
producing what are, in effect, variants of existing image structures. The
process is somewhat similar to the method used in SMOTE for synthetically
generating records for the minority class (see “Data Generation”). Based on
the information in an existing set of images, generative AI can produce
more images that are like them. These images can provide more variation
than the original set. For example, using different colors, lighting, or
orientation produces variation that gives a classification algorithm a richer
training set. This can be particularly useful in augmenting data for
underrepresented classes.
Naturally there are limitations to the effectiveness of artificially generated
training data. The algorithm producing them only “knows” the existing
data, so if there are significant gaps in how that existing data represents the
real world, those gaps will propagate to the artificial data. In addition, the
generation process may introduce noise or artifacts that muddy the task of
the classification algorithm being trained. This is particularly a problem
when the process of using AI-generated data in training is not carefully
specified and controlled. More and more AI-generated content is prevalent
on the web, and may be swept up unknowingly into training data. This
degrades model accuracy, sometimes so much that the model loses
effectiveness (termed model collapse). The circular process of generating
data and then training models on that generated data is termed data
cannibalization.
Integrating Generative AI into Applications
Any individual can use generative AI by opening a free account at one of
the generative AI providers and typing in a query. Effective business use of
generative AI requires integration into specific applications. For example, a
consumer-facing business seeking to improve customer support will need a
model that is tuned for its specific needs (say, dealing with airline
reservation problems), and integrated seamlessly into its existing operations
(say, producing voice responses in response to voice queries, or written
responses to inquiries received by chat). Most providers of generative AI
services offer API connections to their models so that company developers
can do the coding, e.g., to engineer the automated production of prompts, as
discussed above. These providers offer a variety of other custom solutions,
such as software-as-a-service (SaaS), as well as support and consultation.
This is an important revenue generation model for generative AI, so these
products and services will become deeper and more comprehensive, and
probably more expensive. In addition to standalone and custom
deployment, popular tools available to both lay and technical people are
beginning to integrate generative AI capabilities. Microsoft’s Office 365
suite of office tools now includes links to Copilot, its generative AI facility.
GitHub, the programming environment owned by Microsoft, also has a
Copilot facility to guide and autocomplete coding. Google likewise has
generative AI integration plans for its Workspace set of tools.
Agentic AI
Over time, AI is moving from comparatively simple tasks (predict demand
for air travel for the next six months) to more complex tasks (make short-
term airline operational decisions, e.g., crew and aircraft scheduling). This
involves taking directions at a relatively high level, and generating the
needed lower-level tasks and actions to achieve the overall goal. This is
called agentic AI, where AI is acting as an agent in pursuit of a human-
specified goal. A simple example is a calendar coordination agent that
multiple people might direct to find a mutually acceptable meeting time.
Agentic systems often integrate with tools, APIs, or real-world sensors so
they can incorporate the needed context, such as calendar availability,
inventory levels and locations, weather forecasts, and, of course, user
feedback.
Task Decomposition and Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
The process of breaking a complex goal into simpler subproblems is known
as task decomposition. In LLMs, this is encouraged at the interface level
through chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, causing the model to generate a
sequence of intermediate steps rather than jumping directly to a final
answer. Prompting with phrases such as “let’s think step by step,” or by
including an example solution that shows intermediate reasoning, cues the
model to produce these intermediate steps rather than collapsing
prematurely to a short, heuristic answer. CoT prompting does not reveal
internal computations, rather the apparent multistep reasoning shown to the
user serves as a linguistic scaffold. CoT prompting has been shown to
significantly improve performance on complex tasks such as mathematical
reasoning, logic puzzles, and multistep inference.
The Generative AI Stack
In contrast to earlier machine learning methods, generative AI encompasses
a rich, multilayered ecosystem. The generative AI stack can be
conceptualized in several layers:
Infrastructure layer
GPUs provide the basic computational infrastructure. These powerful
processors facilitate parallel processing and gained their name from the
video applications they were originally designed for. Tensor processing
units (TPUs) and other specialized accelerators are also used.
Distributed computing frameworks manage the large-scale computation.
Foundation models layer
Large pretrained models (GPT, Claude, Gemini, LLaMA, and more)
provide the essential language capability and knowledge of the world.
Customization layer
Further training and processing yields models that are suited to specific
applications and domains.
Application layer
End-user tools (chatbots, copilots, creative tools, medical assistants)
provide interfaces for human use, usually accessing models through
APIs (application programming interfaces) that expose model
capabilities to software developers.
Interoperability and orchestration layer
Tools that integrate multiple models and services to create complex
workflows (e.g., LangChain, LlamaIndex).
KEY IDEAS
The most powerful near-term uses of generative AI are likely
to be incremental improvements on existing processes.
Generative AI can augment scarce training data by introducing
variation.
Agentic AI translates high-level prompts (that convey
objectives) into subtasks.
LLMs can use chain-of-thought prompting to generate
intermediate responses and improve performance.
Generative AI encompasses a multilayered ecosystem or
“stack” from infrastructure to applications.
Further Reading
“ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models,” by Yao
et al., 2022, on combining reasoning and action in LLMs
Summary
Generative AI is a class of neural net models that have the ability to
produce text, images, audio, or video on the basis of a user-supplied prompt
(a written query, request, or instruction). Large language models (LLMs),
extensions of autocomplete models that predict the next word, are trained
and operate at a massive scale. They require computing infrastructure
orders of magnitude greater than prior machine learning models. The key
innovation that enabled this leap in functionality, requiring, in turn, a
corresponding jump in computing power, is the attention mechanism. This
allows the model to focus on parts of the input that are relevant, not just the
parts that are nearby in sequence. Foundation models such as GPT are
pretrained on massive amounts of text data from the internet, learning
grammar, vocabulary, and syntax. These models can be adapted to specific
domains via fine-tuning (further training on domain-specific data) or
retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), which directs the model to
reference a specific data set without further training. Models that generate
images or videos use similar attention mechanisms and are trained on large
data sets of labeled images using diffusion methods that iteratively refine
random noise into coherent images. At this writing, generative AI is still
evolving. In the next chapter we explore questions and concerns about its
future use.
Exploration with AI
Ask an LLM to explain attention and how it works, in contrast to
RNNs. Compare the answers from two different LLMs. Which do
you find clearer and more useful? Why?
Compile a short description of the features of a product with which
you are familiar, and ask an LLM to generate a marketing blurb for
it. Now set a higher temperature for the LLM (you may need to use
an API for this) and ask it to generate a second blurb. Do this again
with a yet higher temperature. Compare the three results and
describe the differences.
Describe tasks for which you might prefer a higher or lower
temperature.
What are the advantages and disadvantages of fine-tuning versus
RAG for adapting a general LLM to a specific domain? Give an
example of a situation in which you would prefer one over the
other.
The field of AI is rapidly evolving. Research a topic in this chapter
and describe the extent to which new developments have occurred
since the writing of this chapter, and the extent to which they have
become widely adopted. (If none, research earlier approaches to
the topic and why they were superseded.)
We didn’t provide a detailed explanation of how the temperature
parameter works. Ask your AI to explain how the temperature
parameter modifies the softmax function and how it influences the
sampling process during text generation.

This chapter draws on material written by Peter Bruce, Galit Shmueli, and Peter Gedeck,
copyright 2024 Datastats LLC, used by permission.
