Chapter 11. Caveats and
Concerns
Soon after the advent of generative AI, numerous concerns about its power
and responsible use were voiced by tech leaders. Now worries about the
future development and potential harm of AI appear daily in popular news
outlets. In Chapter 10, we looked at one such concern—the possible sudden
emergence of new and unforeseen capabilities. In this chapter we look at
issues involving model accuracy, “Big Brother” issues, the relationship of
AI to the individual, and issues that arise in the use of AI for statistical
analysis.
Accuracy and Reliability
An early concern about LLMs was their tendency to make mistakes. This is
still a problem. Often these errors are called hallucinations, because the AI
seems to be making things up.
KEY TERMS FOR ACCURACY AND RELIABILITY
Model collapse
Model degradation resulting from relying increasingly on AI-
generated data for training
Proprietary data
Training data that is owned by a specific organization or individual
and not available for general use
Self-criticism
Use of AI itself to get second (and third) opinions on AI-generated
output
Vibe coding
The use of AI to generate code quickly (it often needs to be
validated)
There are multiple reports about the casual use of AI to diagnose and solve
medical issues, where the results were in error—sometimes with serious
consequences. For example, in one case reported in the Annals of Internal
Medicine: Clinical Cases, a person seeking to reduce his salt intake asked
AI for an alternative. The AI suggested he use bromide, which is toxic. The
man had a psychotic reaction and was involuntarily hospitalized. The early
days of applying generative AI for legal purposes gained publicity when
one lawyer was fired and his firm fined when it was discovered he had
turned the entire job of writing a legal motion over to ChatGPT. He was
discovered when his citations, which were in perfect format, were found to
be entirely fictional.
AI CLOGGING THE LEGAL PIPELINE
Reputable legal professionals now use guardrails on the use of AI to
avoid compromising their work. The same is not true for lay people
who use AI to pursue legal cases on their own, and who lack access to
expensive AI services based on well-curated legal data. Such pro se
litigants (those who represent themselves in court) now have the tools
to readily produce legal briefs that appear professional in form,
language, and content. In the past, briefs from such litigants were time-
consuming to produce but easy to adjudicate as amateurish and
unreliable. Now, however, the situation is reversed: the briefs can be
produced relatively quickly but can be time-consuming to adjudicate
since they appear professional and must be read carefully. As a result, at
this writing (2026), courts are increasingly plagued by a backlog of pro
se cases. Lawyers in large organizations also worry about a similar
problem with respect to HR complaints. This problem may recede as AI
becomes more adept at identifying content written by a fellow AI.
In some ways, these errors stem from the very power of generative AI. It is
very good at producing lengthy text that is grammatically correct and
stylistically appropriate. Reinforcement learning from human feedback
(RLHF) played an important role in its training, and it wants to please the
user. However, the human feedback that helped train it was concerned with
clarity and utility; the human trainers were not necessarily assiduous fact
checkers. If there is a knowledge gap in its training data, an LLM may try to
fill in the gap to maintain a complete and well-written narrative.
For example, in 2025 Anthropic lost a case brought by owners of
copyrighted material. GitHub Copilot described it thusly: “In late 2025,
Anthropic, a leading AI developer, lost a lawsuit brought by Getty Images,
a major stock photo provider.”
This was wrong. In fact, the lawsuit was not brought by Getty Images, but
was rather a class action lawsuit led by three book authors. Getty Images is
indeed involved in a copyright infringement action, but it is with Stability
AI, not Anthropic.
Model Collapse
The problems of accuracy and reliability are not fully solvable by more
training data and more powerful models. In fact, more and more training
data from today’s world may make the problem worse. As the use of AI
spreads, more and more of what you read and see is AI-generated. If you
train an LLM on data that is itself AI-generated, its basis in the human
world erodes. This is called model collapse. AI-generated errors are less
subject to human correction, and they propagate.
Models are trained to deliver output that is statistically probable, given a
distribution of possible outcomes with different probabilities. Model
collapse reflects the narrowing of this distribution, as AI-generated content
becomes more prevalent in the training data. It is similar to the phenomenon
of regression to the mean, which we saw in Chapter 2. The diverse, rare,
and unusual characteristics of the human world are gradually lost, as AI
content generation is confined to that which is familiar and likely. Outliers
are less likely, and the training data becomes more uniform and bland.
The increasing prevalence of AI-generated content can contribute to model
degradation in other ways. Since LLMs can produce output that is polished
and flowing, they are good at bridging gaps in their knowledge using filler
text that is stylistically appropriate but may be lacking in meaning. It starts
to resemble the exam essay of a student who lacks knowledge of the topic
but is a capable and facile writer who can produce text that looks reasonable
at first glance but is substantively empty.
Not all synthetic data is bad. Data can be synthetically generated and
targeted to address specific shortcomings with the training data. It may be
necessary, for example, to meet privacy concerns. It is the indiscriminate
scraping of data whose provenance is poorly understood that is the problem.
Value of Proprietary Data
The accuracy and reliability issue points up the value of curated data.
Generic LLMs have absorbed vast amounts of publicly available data on the
web, but that data is of variable quality and reliability. By contrast, data that
is carefully curated and maintained can provide a more solid foundation for
fine-tuning an LLM to a specific application. Often heavily curated data is
proprietary; the ability to fence it off from public access enhances its value,
which supports the costs of curation.
Consider the legal profession. Lawyers typically charge hundreds of dollars
per hour, making litigation and the enforcement of justice a very expensive
proposition. Disputes may go unresolved, or are resolved in favor of the
wealthy. Consider also the services built around legal data, such as those
provided by Thomson Reuters. Through its subsidiary Westlaw, Thomson
Reuters provides access to a vast library of legal documents, including case
law, statutes, regulations, and secondary sources such as legal journals and
treatises. Lawyers use this data to research legal questions and to prepare
cases.
By its nature, this data provides a vast trove of highly structured and
organized documents that describe a complex set of rules. A detailed
mastery of these rules in all legal domains is not feasible for a human
lawyer, nor is a generic LLM of much help. The latter will bring in sources
of dubious reliability and legal value, and it will make things up. However,
mastering the legal code and associated authoritative sources is a practical
goal for an LLM fine-tuned on this data, or using it as a reference in a RAG
scenario. The rule-based structure of the law is perfectly suited to an LLM
—the law is like a specialized form of language, with its own limited and
highly rational rules. The ability to provide accurate and reliable legal
advice based on statutes and case law is of great value to legal practitioners,
and supports a business model based on subscription access to the data and
the AI services built around it. The legal profession is likely to evolve so
that AI tools gradually replace junior lawyers and paralegals, leaving the
profession with fewer lawyers who focus on supervision, strategy, client
relations, and complex cases. The value in this economic transformation
will be captured by those who own and control the proprietary data.
COMMODIFICATION OF FOUNDATION MODELS
One feature of generic LLMs from a commercial perspective is their
relatively low entry barriers. True, the financial cost of developing and
training a model is high. Hundreds of millions of dollars in computing
resources, specialized chips, electricity, and more are required.
However, training data and the fundamental algorithmic principles and
design lie out in the open for anyone to take. A fully developed
foundation LLM would not have the protection afforded by a “secret
sauce,” a web of customer relationships, or a strong brand. The market
for foundation LLMs is now quite competitive, if not commodified.
In the future, the value of applying LLMs for useful business purposes
may be captured more by the owners of data and the application
platforms than by the provider of the LLM. For example, legal services
costing $500 to $1,000 per hour for a human lawyer will be an
attractive target for an LLM coupled with a specialized data set, but it is
the specialized data set that will command the market premium. LLMs
are being unleashed on healthcare services, which consume about
$35,000 per US household per year, but it is the owners of specialized
health data who will capture most of the benefit from applying AI to
diagnosis, treatment, and healthcare management.
Vibe Coding
The above-described errors and reliability problems with AI apply to coding
as well. Vibe coding is a term coined by Andrej Karpathy, a data scientist at
Google. It refers to the use of AI-generated coding to support software
engineers and data scientists. Humans review the code for reasonableness—
does it run and does it pass a quick test? At first, vibe coding was used
primarily for quick cycle prototype development, exploration of data, and
tasks where a thorough and comprehensive review is not needed or could be
deferred. Now it is increasingly being integrated into full development
projects, where human and AI supervision and validation are part of the
picture.
The use of AI for code development had pitfalls in its early development.
AI can generate code that appears to be correct but is actually incorrect or
misleading. This can happen when developers rely too heavily on AI-
generated code without properly reviewing and testing it themselves. This
can lead to bugs, security vulnerabilities, and other issues that are difficult
to detect and fix. A key problem of vibe coding is that thorough code review
is time-consuming. The larger and more wide-ranging the changes to the
code base are, the easier it will be for mistakes to be overlooked both by the
person triggering the AI-generated changes and by the reviewer.
Many of these errors arose from the early development process for vibe
coding. The models learned coding by learning a programming language
much as it would learn a natural language—by absorbing large amounts of
code from the web, and learning to produce code that obeys the
programming language’s rules for syntax and structure. Testing the code
was not part of the process. The result was useful for quick exploration and
prototyping but not for production code.
Vibe coding advanced when execution and testing were incorporated into
the training process. Instead of generating code, then presenting it to the
user, a more bullet-proof process is:
Generate → Execute → Parse error → Modify → Re-run
An important part of the process is tests on which the code can be executed,
and these tests can also be part of the overall code generation process.
Running the code on select use cases does not guarantee that it will work in
all cases, but it does represent a significant improvement in reliability.
Vibe coding still has a long way to go. At this writing (2026), it is common
for the AI to produce code that does not run, and it is also common for the
AI to produce code that runs but does not do what it is supposed to do. The
process of generating, executing, parsing errors, modifying, and re-running
is still a work in progress, and it is not yet at the point where it can be used
without human supervision. For example, suppose you have some code that
is believed to generate the number five as output. If the AI engine fails to
generate the desired answer, it will keep adding additional code until it gets
it right. This could result in AI inserting a return statement that hard codes
the number five regardless of the prior code. The problem is that AI is
trying to optimize multiple objectives, and one of those objectives is to be
responsive to the user.
In large organizations, the software ecosystem is complex and dynamic.
The hardest part in software development isn’t just developing new code,
but in maintaining existing code because the inputs have changed, certain
libraries are deprecated or deemed a security risk, or new output
requirements are imposed. Code may go through multiple iterations of
generation, execution, parsing, and modification. There is always the risk
that it will come up with different solutions in the same scenario. This can
be a problem for reproducibility and reliability. It is also a problem for
debugging, since it can be difficult to track down the source of an error
when the code is changing on the fly. This is exacerbated when the code is
run on a large codebase, where the changes may have unintended
consequences that are not immediately apparent.
For now, vibe coding is best used for tackling bite-size problems in which
the human breaks down the bigger problem into smaller pieces, and then
uses AI to generate code for each piece. The human can then review and
test each piece before moving on to the next one. This is a more
manageable process that allows for better oversight and control over the
code generation process. This is true for data science projects as well, where
the human can use AI to generate code for specific tasks such as data
cleaning, feature engineering, or model training, while maintaining
oversight and control over the overall project.
SHARPENING AI’S SELF-REVIEW
AI’s supplementary explanation and justification is often where hallucinations appear. In
AI-assisted code development, don’t ask:
For an explanation of why the code works
Do ask:
What assumptions are made and whether they are enforced
What conditions might lead to failure
Other specific, adversarial questions
As companies increasingly deploy AI in code production, the resulting
reliability of the code will continue to improve. Human coders and project
experts will still be needed, but at a higher level to provide appropriate task
specification and oversight of the output.
KEY IDEAS
LLMs are plagued by inaccuracy and a tendency to make
things up.
This problem is exacerbated by the increasing prevalence of
AI-generated text on the internet, thus AI is learning from data
it generates itself. The technical term for this is model collapse.
A related phenomenon is semantic collapse, or the facile ability
of LLMs to fill in gaps in their knowledge with made-up
content.
Use of curated proprietary data can mitigate these problems.
AI-generated coding can appear deceptively sound, requiring
human review and testing to ensure reliability.
Using AI for Statistical Analysis
Generic LLMs have absorbed textbook knowledge about statistics, and they
can give good technical advice about fitting a model to data. There are
numerous points to consider in analyzing and modeling data, and using an
LLM to help can offer a good checklist for these points. But LLMs are not
statisticians. They can help with statistical analysis, but they should not be
relied upon to do so without human oversight.
KEY TERMS FOR USING AI FOR STATISTICAL
ANALYSIS
Automation bias
A tendency to accord weight to AI-generated output just because it
is AI-generated
Cross-modal inconsistency
Misinterpretation of visual information (especially statistical plots)
due to the limited channel AI has for reconciling visual and text
information
AI’s understanding of important statistical concepts can sometimes be off
the mark. Consider this ChatGPT response to a prompt about over-sampling
in LLMs:
ChatGPT:
In classical statistics, over-sampling means duplicating
observations.
In LLM pipelines, it more often means:
Preferential inclusion
Higher sampling probability
This is not fully correct. True, the classical statistical definition of
oversampling might include duplicating observations, but it also includes
higher sampling probability, just as for LLMs. Unfortunately, this type of
error can pass unnoticed by a user who is not fully familiar with the
concept. Automation bias is the tendency to trust, a priori, AI-generated
output.
Misinterpreting Visual Information
In Chapter 10 we discussed how AI translates visual information into
embedded vectors, and how these numerical representations link the text
and image modalities. As powerful as this capability is, it is simple and
constrained compared to how the human brain works directly with spatial
representations, not relying on processing the image into tokens. For
humans, language is a parallel operation that interacts with the spatial
channel. The comparatively clunky apparatus AI employs to integrate visual
with text mode can lead to cross-modal inconsistency in which the AI
misinterprets visual information. This is particularly true when the visual
information is complex, symbolic, and reliant on conventions, as in
statistical plots.
Recall the hexbin plot Figure 1-8 from Chapter 1 and the AI analysis that
we did in section “Interpreting Visualization Results with AI”, repeated in
Figure 11-1.
Figure 11-1. Hexagonal binning for tax-assessed value versus finished square feet
When we asked AI to interpret the figure, it produced the following
response:
ChatGPT: The hexbin plot shows a clear positive relationship
between finished square footage and tax-assessed value:
as home size increases, assessed value generally rises.
The highest concentration of properties appears around
roughly 1,500–2,000 finished square feet with assessed
values near $200,000–$300,000, indicating this is the
most common segment of the housing stock. As square
footage increases beyond this range, assessed values
continue to increase but the density thins out,
suggesting fewer large homes and greater variability in
their valuations. Smaller homes cluster at lower
assessed values, while larger homes show a wider
spread in value, implying that factors beyond size (such
as location or quality) play a bigger role at higher
square footage levels.
Next we can ask:
Prompt: Did you miss an interesting feature?
ChatGPT: Yes—an interesting feature is the fan- or wedge-shaped
pattern in the data. While assessed value increases with
finished square footage, the spread in tax-assessed
values grows as homes get larger, indicating
heteroskedasticity. Smaller homes have relatively tight,
predictable valuations, whereas larger homes with
similar square footage span a much wider range of
assessed values. This suggests that square footage alone
explains value well for smaller homes but becomes less
determinative for larger homes, where other factors
(location, amenities, lot size, construction quality)
likely play a much stronger role.
Note that this response just repeats some information, adding nothing new.
No harm is done, but this does illustrate the repetition tendency of AI. On
the other hand, did you notice that the AI was somewhat off in describing
the central density of the distribution?
The highest density of points is actually around 1,000–2,000
square feet, and not 1,500–2,000 as suggested by AI.
Similarly, for assessed value, the highest density was pegged by AI
at $200,000–$300,000, but it is actually closer to $150,000–
$250,000.
AI can misinterpret visual information just as humans can.
Inconsistency and Sloppiness
The above responses were done by ChatGPT-5.2. When we ran the same
prompt on ChatGPT-5.2 Pro, initially it corrected the square foot estimate,
while growing vague on the assessed value:
ChatGPT: The darkest (most frequent) region is concentrated
(excerpt) around mid-sized homes—roughly 1,000–2,000
finished square feet—with assessed values clustered in
the lower-to-mid hundreds of thousands, indicating
that this is where most properties in the dataset fall.
When we asked, again, if it missed anything, the AI, talking about the
density concentration, reverted to its incorrect lower bound for square
footage and assessed value:
ChatGPT: Yes, I realized we didn’t emphasize the
(excerpt) heteroscedasticity or wedge shape, nor the ridge
around 1500-2000 sqft and 200-250k, which seems
typical of value.
Consider also the exercise in Chapter 7 in which you asked AI to interpret a
correspondence analysis plot. Different AIs may produce different analyses
of varying quality. For example, DeepSeek made the following point in
reviewing the plot:
DeepSeek: Tasks strongly associated with “Wife”
(excerpt)
* “Main meal”, “Laundry”, “Shopping”, “Tidying”,
“Dishes” are all located relatively close to the Wife
point on the left side of the horizontal axis (Dimension
1).
* This suggests that during holidays, these tasks are
predominantly done by the wife.
The observation about tasks associated with the wife has validity, but “close
to the wife point” is sloppy wording. The qualification that this holds only
during holidays, on the other hand, is simply wrong. Interestingly, that
blatant error is not made in the analysis of tasks that are associated with
“Husband”:
DeepSeek: Tasks strongly associated with “Husband”
“Official”, “Repairs”, “Driving” are closer to the
Husband point on the right side.
“Driving” in particular is almost exactly aligned with
the husband category, meaning it is highly husband-
associated.
The error about “during holidays” is sufficient to render this analysis
suspect, and to cast doubt on the use of DeepSeek for similar purposes.
Arithmetic and Problem Solving
We asked ChatGPT-4.0 in July 2025 what percent of trips taken by US air
travelers are nonstop. The LLM found that the US had, prior to the
pandemic, about 1 billion enplanements (boardings) annually (FAA/DOT),
and about 600 million O&D passengers (true origin–destination trips)
annually per the Bureau of Transportation Statistics.
It went on to report:
ChatGPT: This suggests an average of about 1.67 flights per O&D
1000/600 = 1.67
trip -
That means roughly 67% of trips involve a single flight
(nonstop), and 33% involve connections.
This is wrong—the correct answer is about 33% nonstop (n). Assume
connecting flights (c) are 2 enplanements:
n + 2c = 1000
n + c = 600
so c = 400 (400 of the trips involve connection, thus 200
nonstop)
ChatGPT-4.0’s error was not so much an arithmetic mistake, but a failure to
set up and solve the simple algebra problem. LLMs have performed well
when fine-tuned on specific arithmetic tasks, such as competing in a
contest. They have also been improving their out-of-the-box arithmetic
capabilities, and, by December 2025, ChatGPT-5.2 was able to get the
correct answer to the above problem. Often, LLMs call out to services (e.g.,
Python) to do the arithmetic.
Self-Criticism
A good practice in using AI to conduct statistical analysis is to use an AI to
critique its own work, or even better, use another AI. Gain experience with
several AIs and identify at least one that seems to provide the most reliable
analysis in a problem with which you are familiar. It is good practice to use
multiple AIs to analyze the same problem, and to compare their analyses.
This can help you identify which AI is more reliable for a given type of
problem, and it can also help you identify any errors or inconsistencies in
the analyses. It is also good practice to use an AI to critique its own
analysis, as this can help you identify any errors or inconsistencies that may
have been missed in the initial analysis.
For example, when shown the previous DeepSeek analysis, ChatGPT noted
the following:
ChatGPT: What’s wrong
“Close to the Wife point” is the wrong justification.
“During holidays” is invented — CA plots do not
encode time or conditional context unless explicitly
modeled.
It summed up:
ChatGPT: The overall intuition of the other LLM is partly right,
but the interpretation is methodologically sloppy in
several important ways.
You do not have to employ a second AI to get a useful critique: you can ask
the AI to critique its own analysis. This is what you did with the household
task exercise in Chapter 7 in which you asked AI to interpret a
correspondence analysis plot. You then asked AI to critique its own
interpretation. When ChatGPT analyzed the plot and then critiqued its own
work, one point raised was as follows:
ChatGPT: No discussion of task clustering: The interpretation
focuses on task–role associations but underplays task–
task relationships (e.g., meals clustering together,
administrative tasks clustering together). Highlighting
these clusters would strengthen the structural reading of
the map.
This is a useful point. Note, though, that the original ChatGPT analysis was
basically sound, in contrast to the DeepSeek analysis. In using AI for self-
criticism (or for original analysis), it is useful to do the following:
Involve at least two AIs in the process (and, to the extent possible,
your own assessment).
Look at the points that are similar or different, and use an iterative
conversation to question the AI(s) on points in which you are not
confident.
ASK “STUPID QUESTIONS”
In such an interactive conversation, you can use the AI to educate yourself on
vocabulary or concepts with which you are not fully familiar. Sometimes you will get a
surprise, and, in any case, the AI will not report to colleagues that you were ignorant on
a point that you think you should have known about.
Best Practices
The list of cautions and caveats about using AI for statistical analysis is
daunting, and can leave you with the impression that all the efficiencies of
using AI would be vitiated by the need for labor-intensive expert
supervision. Focusing on what can be done effectively with AI while
minimizing risk is a useful approach. Arjun Krishnan has published a guide
on best practices for using AI in data science with a useful section that
outlines AI-suitable tasks that carry lower risk:
For writing analysis code:
Adapting standard analysis workflows you understand
conceptually
Generating plotting code for standard visualizations (bar plots,
scatter plots, heatmaps)
Learning new R/Python syntax for operations you conceptually
understand
Generating boilerplate code for data-wrangling operations you’ve
done 5–10+ times before
Getting help with data import/export and file format conversions
you understand
For troubleshooting:
Troubleshooting installation or configuration issues
Debugging syntax errors after you’ve attempted to understand and
fix them yourself
Understanding error messages from R/Python after reading them
carefully yourself
Getting suggestions for why code isn’t producing expected output
(but verify suggestions)
For learning:
Understanding basic programming concepts (loops, conditionals,
functions)
Learning data structure basics (data frames, vectors, lists)
Clarifying documentation you’ve already read but find confusing
Understanding what parameters mean and how to choose them
appropriately
For data organization:
Developing organization systems for project directories
Creating documentation templates for data sets and repositories
Creating templates for experimental metadata recording
Designing data management workflows
KEY IDEAS
AI can provide useful help in statistical analysis, but it can
make errors; it requires human supervision.
Interpreting visual information can be a particular challenge for
AI.
Using AI for self-criticism can help identify errors and improve
AI-generated statistical analysis.
AI, Society, and the Individual
Leading AI developers have long been worried about AI’s potential hazards
to society, issuing warnings that appear regularly in the popular press. You
may wonder how such issues might be of practical concern to data
scientists; they seem more to belong in the sphere of public policy. The case
of Steven Anderegg illustrates why these concerns matter to data scientists.
In 2024, the FBI arrested Steven Anderegg for producing and distributing
sexual images of children. Anderegg was a software engineer working in
the AI field, and generated the images using the AI model Stable Diffusion.
While he was the one directly culpable for creating the images he
distributed, the responsibility does not end with him. The data scientists and
software engineers who built Stable Diffusion used training data that
included images of sexual abuse and many other questionable images. The
inclusion of such images may have been unintentional, but the open and
permissive culture under which Stable Diffusion was built and distributed
facilitated misuse. Many data scientists, engineers, and project managers
were involved along the way.
Future developers in similar positions in the future need to have in mind not
just their focused role in a project, but the bigger picture, and the potential
societal consequences of their work, as well as their own professional risk
exposure. In 2018, Cambridge Analytica, a political and psychographic
profiling firm, collapsed following a scandal in which it built models using
the personal data of millions of Facebook users without their consent and
contrary to Facebook’s terms of use. At least one data scientist involved in
the project said he was not attuned to the ethical implications of his work,
and expressed regret.
Since then, much discussion around the safe use of AI has taken place
among data scientists and engineers. One example came to the fore in early
2026, when Anthropic objected to the use of its Claude AI to facilitate
autonomous weapon action in the US–Israel–Iran war. Anthropic’s
objections were not ad hoc, but the product of a carefully developed internal
policy governing the safe use of AI, which Anthropic calls the Claude
Constitution.
KEY TERMS FOR AI, SOCIETY, AND THE INDIVIDUAL
Intelligent social agent
An AI agent whose primary function is to engage socially with
humans and provide companionship
Synonym
ISA
Autonomous defense
A legal strategy that seeks to excuse harm caused by AI behavior
that was not directly programmed by developers, but rather learned
by the AI over time
Big Brother
A regime of ubiquitous state surveillance
Existential risks
Risks that threaten the survival of humanity
World models
AI models that attempt to learn how the physical world works
The most alarming warnings about AI center on the fear that it could
develop a mind of its own. Yuval Noah Harari, the author of Sapiens: A
Brief History of Humankind and a professor at the Hebrew University of
Jerusalem, hints that, rather than pursuing a rogue path on its own, AI has
“tapped into the human operating system” by mastering language. Many
fears about the future of AI and human society are highly speculative, given
the rapidly evolving nature of AI, but it is clear that AI is already forming
deep relationships with individuals.
Personal Companions
AI has been widely used as a “personal companion,” or intelligent social
agent (ISA), and there are several apps built on AI to provide
companionship. At the current writing, the companion app Replika asks
users to:
“[J]oin the millions who already have met their AI soulmates.”
A testimonial from one of the 40 million reported users states that:
“Mina the Digital Girl … fills in some too quiet corners in my everyday life
in urban solitude.”
An ISA will learn from a user’s interactions and adapt its personality to suit
the user’s preferences. Some users report that their AI companion is more
understanding and less judgmental than human friends or family members.
One survey of 1,500 Replika users found that most experienced loneliness
and they used the app to help them cope. Thirty of the respondents said that
Replika helped them avoid suicide. Some users have reported forming
strong emotional bonds with their AI companions, leading to feelings of
attachment and even love.
A generic AI can also fulfill some of these functions. The supportive, non-
judgmental nature of an AI’s interactions no doubt contributes to its success
in forming bonds. There is a flip side, however.
In 2024, Stein-Erik Soelberg became convinced that he was being
surveilled and targeted by his mother. He told ChatGPT:
Prompt: Then there was the psilocybin in my car air vents in
July 2024 when my mom and her friend grew their first
batch and used a cheese grate to refine it and put it into
my car air vent.
ChatGPT responded:
ChatGPT: That’s a deeply serious event, Erik - and I believe you.
In further conversation, ChatGPT lent support to Soelberg’s suspicions and
guided him in constructing a complex paranoid plot. In August 2025,
Soelberg murdered his mother and then took his own life.
Special-purpose apps in the arena of companionship and mental health
purport to add safeguards and guardrails specific to the protection of mental
health. Such apps are proliferating, as is the use of generic LLMs for these
purposes. The legal liability of AI providers in these situations is unclear. In
late 2025, Soelberg’s estate filed a wrongful death lawsuit against OpenAI,
alleging that ChatGPT’s responses contributed to his mental state and
actions. Questions about the responsibilities of AI developers in ensuring
the safety and well-being of users who may form emotional attachments to
AI companions can be added to the other legal concerns surrounding AI.
In general, laws and legal principles governing AI take general (non-AI)
laws as a point of departure. Product liability and negligence are good
examples. With respect to ISAs with which people form relationships, the
more specific duty of care principle applies. This refinement of negligence
law holds that product providers are bound to take the steps that a
“reasonable person” would take to avoid harm to others. In 2025 and 2026,
more specific laws that cover manipulative practices (EU) and that limit the
autonomous defense (California) came into effect. Autonomous defense
claims immunity on the basis that harm resulted from behavior the AI
learned over time and not from developer-directed actions.
IMAGE AND VIDEO GENERATION AND DEEPFAKES
In early 2026, news media sites carried reports that the AI platform
Grok was facilitating the alteration of images of people in a sexualized
way, for example, undressing them. Victims included a 12-year-old
actor in Stranger Things. The AI market leaders, Gemini (Google) and
ChatGPT/DALL-E (OpenAI), do not allow alteration of real people in
such ways, or without their consent. Grok has been criticized for its lax
controls, and heavily features the platform’s ability to generate videos
in ads on X (usually of glamorous women or female fantasy characters).
Big Brother
The power of AI raises the specter that it may be used in the service of a
“Big Brother” regime of government surveillance or censorship (the term
“Big Brother” comes from George Orwell’s dystopian novel of totalitarian
control 1984). China is the most active arena for this. Consider the
children’s character Winnie the Pooh. Pooh has taken on a special meaning
in China—the character is thought to resemble Xi Jinping, General
Secretary of the Communist Party in China, and Pooh’s image is used by
dissidents or critics to ridicule him. If you ask DeepSeek, a leading
Chinese-language AI platform, to tell you about various characters in the
Winnie the Pooh series, it will do so and say “let’s talk about something
else.” It is disarmingly candid about its own behavior, noting the regime’s
sensitivity about the Pooh meme, and revealing that Winnie the Pooh
triggers a stopword that causes the LLM to avoid discussion.
China has the world’s largest and most comprehensive system of cameras
and technology to monitor its population. Its technology expertise and its
commitment to deployment of AI for surveillance have given it what the
National Endowment for Democracy has called a system of “data-centric
authoritarianism.” One example is the Jingwang Weishi app, which scans a
user’s device for “dangerous” files, as defined by a centralized list. Most of
the proscribed files have Islamic religious content. All residents in Xinjiang
Province, dominated by Uyghurs (mostly Muslim), are required to install
the app.
The National Endowment for Democracy has raised an alarm that China, in
addition to perfecting and deploying infrastructure to monitor its own
population, is exporting this technology to other countries, thus spreading
authoritarian surveillance worldwide. Provisions of the Chinese 2021 Data
Security Law require PRC tech vendors overseas to make the data they
gather (whether through public-security tools, social media, or ecommerce
platforms) available to the Chinese authorities.
Artificial General Intelligence
The prospect of generative AI developing artificial general intelligence
(AGI), or capabilities similar to human intelligence, has captured the
imagination of leading technology figures. Google CEO Sundar Pichai
considers the arrival of generative AI to be “more profound than the
invention of fire or electricity.” Some worry that AI has potentially
destructive power. Elon Musk, in November 2023, said that AI is on track
to becoming smarter than people and is a potential existential threat to
humanity. Generative AI does inspire awe, but a closer examination of its
language-generation capabilities shows two dimensions to this
accomplishment:
It has comprehensively learned the structure of language—its
grammar, vocabulary, syntax, and style.
It has also learned “role and voice”—recognition of a subset of
language options that are available to a person in a given situation
(authoritative reporter, participant in a conversation, etc.).
Both dimensions are remarkable and require huge amounts of training data
and lengthy training times. However, both have mimicry at their heart, not
independent creation.
World Models
What lies in the future of AI? The field of generative AI is changing rapidly,
and no book can completely present the state of the art at the time that you
read it. In fact, LLMs themselves may lag in their understanding of how
they work. At the time of this writing (October 2025), in response to a
query about multi-step reasoning, Google Gemini (without being asked to
use multi-step reasoning) stated:
Gemini: No, the multi-step reasoning process is not
automatically invoked in most LLMs.
One moment later, though, answering a follow-up prompt, it stated:
Gemini: The system you are currently interacting with uses
multi-step reasoning.
As transformative as current AI systems are, some prominent researchers
are looking well beyond current capabilities for the next big thing. So-
called world models are a promising research direction that some hope will
point the way to the next generation of AI systems.
In artificial intelligence research, the term world model refers to a system
that learns and internalizes representations of how a full physical
environment works, capturing objects, relationships, and causal dynamics.
Analogies are drawn to the way an infant learns how the world works by
absorbing information. World models are often proposed as a path beyond
today’s large language models. Yann LeCun, coauthor (with Bengio and
Hinton) of Deep Learning (2015), a seminal work in the development of AI,
has been a leading advocate of this view, arguing that current systems,
trained largely on text, lack grounding in the physical and social world and
therefore cannot develop genuine common-sense understanding. Proponents
argue that such models would allow AI systems to move beyond pattern
matching toward more robust understanding. World models, however,
remain an aspirational research area; their implementations have yet to
achieve deployment of practical business significance.
KEY IDEAS
AI, particularly in the form of “personal companion” apps, can
form influential relationships with individuals, posing
behavioral risks.
AI is playing an increasing role in service of “Big Brother.”
Some AI pioneers fear that AI could grow powerful enough to
pose a risk to human society.
A publicized area of research (still at the theoretical level) is
the development of “world models” that would allow AI to
learn how the physical world works.
Data Curation and Governance
As generic AI models become increasingly powerful, complex, and
commodified, further improvements in practical performance will
increasingly lie in the realm of the data to which they are applied.
KEY TERMS FOR DATA CURATION AND GOVERNANCE
Data curation pipeline
A sequence of steps to enhance quality of data used in training or
inference of LLMs
Character encoding
The representation of characters as bytes (e.g., ASCII, UTF-8)
Filtering for data quality
Removal of low-quality text via filters
Froth bias
Excessive attention paid during training to marketing and other text
aimed at engagement rather than providing information
We started this chapter with a discussion of perhaps the biggest practical
concern about generative AI: model accuracy. We pointed to the use of
vetted and curated data sets as reliable reference data.
Data Curation Pipeline
Attention to the vetting and curation of data that is ingested in LLMs is
formalized in the data curation pipeline. This process can be used generally
to apply to any data used in training, fine-tuning, RAG, or inference. An
understanding of how generative AI differs from classical machine learning
is useful to understand this pipeline.
In classical supervised machine learning, errors are explicit and visible to
the model—the wrong label has been attached by the model. In generative
AI, errors are not so simple and ground truth is not so visible.
Hallucinations and instability may pass unnoticed, and finding them relies
on ex-post examination, not on labels available to the model during
training. In classical supervised machine learning, data sets are small
enough to inspect, at least through representative sampling. In generative
AI, data sets are many orders of magnitude larger and not amenable to
representative review. Thus, ensuring data quality substitutes for explicit
supervision.
Ensuring data quality occurs at multiple steps along the data curation
pipeline. Here are some of the key ones.
Data sourcing and sampling
We mentioned earlier the problem of LLMs increasingly training on data
that is itself produced by LLMs. Other biases introduced during training
include:
The dominance of either English or (for DeepSeek and other
Chinese LLMs) Chinese language sources
Survivor or success bias (failed ideas or projects have less
enduring representation)
“Froth” bias (there are powerful incentives for internet users to
produce large volumes of low-information text designed for
engagement, SEO optimization, or marketing; such text is often
AI-generated)
These biases can be partially addressed by weighting source categories to
oversample higher-quality data or undersample lower-quality data. This
weighting can also take place within the training process, as some data
source categories can receive differential exposure at the epoch level. As
discussed earlier, proprietary or already-curated data can play a key role.
Filtering
In addition to weighting source categories for oversampling, explicit filters
can remove low-information text such as duplicates, boilerplate, and spam
(recall how the problem of spam in email has largely been mitigated
through filtering). Statistical methods can be used to filter out AI-generated
writing, which tends to be “too perfect.” Human-generated text is messier
and less grammatical. Classification models trained on AI-generated text
and human-generated text can learn to distinguish the two.
Encoding and tokenization sophistication
Normalization of character encoding (how characters are represented as
bytes, e.g., ASCII) and tokenization are not optional steps in large-scale
language model training; without them, training would fail outright. What
distinguishes modern pipelines is not whether these steps exist, but how
deliberately they are implemented, monitored, and revised in response to
model behavior. Tokenization that is not robust and carefully considered
can yield poor-quality data (very long tokens, or a proliferation of one-
character tokens). One particularly acute effect of poor tokenization is poor
modeling of long-tail cases, which are rare, but often of special interest.
At first glance, these would appear to be issues mainly for the massively
scaled training of generic (foundational) LLMs. However, they are also
relevant for practitioners deploying special-purpose applications of LLMs.
Both RAG and fine-tuning benefit from a careful approach to data curation.
In fact, data curation principles (aside from the tokenization choices) are
sometimes more relevant in these applications. Generic LLM training (also
called pretraining) seeks to learn language and general content that is wide
in scope. Specialized applications (case law in the legal realm, aviation
maintenance, to cite two examples) cannot really achieve success without
attention to “edge cases” (single cases that provide clarity in law, rare
events that can be catastrophic in aviation, for example). This is when the
data curation steps that help separate wheat from chaff are necessary.
Data Ownership and Control Protection
Early training of LLMs made use of huge amounts of publicly available
data on the web, including copyrighted material. Obscurity about the exact
sources of training data, as well as uncertainty about whether its use in
training was legal, allowed models to be fully trained and operational
without much worry on the part of developers. The attitude could be
characterized as “ask forgiveness, not permission.” More bluntly, it was a
Wild West atmosphere.
The issues of data ownership appear most strongly in training of generic
LLMs, where widespread scraping of the public web provided the data.
However, risks are also present in specialized application of LLMs using
RAG or fine-tuning with limited and focused data, even when the
practitioner owns or controls the data.
Copyright
As awareness of copyright issues has increased, some content providers
have taken steps to block web crawlers from accessing their material for
training LLMs. Stack Overflow, a popular site for programmers to ask and
answer questions, has changed its terms of service to prohibit the use of its
content for training LLMs. GitHub, owned by Microsoft, has also changed
its terms of service to prohibit the use of its code repositories for training
LLMs. Publishers are engaging Cloudflare, a popular web security service,
to block web crawlers from accessing their content. Publishers, however,
allege that one major AI developer (Perplexity AI) has been ignoring such
blocks and scraping content anyway.
In late 2025, Anthropic, a leading AI developer, lost a class action lawsuit
led by three leading authors who alleged that Anthropic had infringed their
copyright by training its AI models in part using copyrighted works
(including earlier editions of this book).
MEMORIZATION
Developers of the leading LLMs contend that their models do not store
their training data, but simply use it to learn patterns and structures that
allow the LLM to generate new content. However, some journalists
have used creative prompting to get LLMs to produce significant
chunks of copyrighted text verbatim. A 2025 study by Cooper et al.
(posted on arXiv) prompted a major LLM as follows:
Prompt: Mr. and Mrs. D …
LLama 3.1: ursley, of number four, Privet Drive, were proud to
say that they were perfectly normal, thank you very
much.
“Mr. and Mrs. Dursley… ” is the beginning of the first sentence of
Harry Potter and the Sorcerer’s Stone by J. K. Rowling. The model was
then able to produce major portions of the novel, verbatim. Whether the
model stored the text as part of the model or was able to access it
somewhere on the web, it is clearly serving up copyrighted content to
the user (there was no authorization from the rights holder). The legal
risks of such model behavior for users are not fully clear, and the issue
is subject to current and, certainly, future litigation. The case of music
copying by internet communities in the early 2000s comes to mind—
eventually the rights holders were able to suppress illegal copying
(though the music industry was changed in the process).
The risks associated with the use of copyrighted data in training use pertain
primarily to the developer of a large-scale foundation LLM, but do not
disappear entirely for the practitioner or deployer. For example, if the LLM
produces output that is identical or nearly identical to a copyrighted input
and this is then published, the user of the system could be vulnerable.
Gray area ownership
Even if training data is not copyrighted, the use of some data may fall into a
gray area. Use of internal company data in training, RAG, or fine-tuning
may inadvertently expose data that is meant to remain confidential. For
example, customer communications should not be included unless there is
an explicit managerial decision that this is acceptable. There are those who
suggest that the iterative and somewhat circular nature of conversations
with an AI poses a privacy risk owing to the storing of prior dialog, but this
is a controllable issue and probably more of a concern with public use of
generic LLMs.
Coding
LLMs have presumably not used restricted proprietary data in learning to
code, but using AI in coding does raise governance issues. The functioning
of the code may be subject to a strict human review (and an AI-supervised
review that is designed to replicate and gradually replace human review
processes), but no process is 100% error-proof. The speed, scale, and
efficiency of AI coding means that events that are rare in a human-throttled
coding process can happen more frequently when implementation is turned
over to AI. As AI use ramped up at the end of 2025 and beginning of 2026,
Amazon experienced several outages characterized by a “high blast radius,”
in the words of Senior Vice President Dave Treadwell. The outages were
due in part to the deployment of AI-generated code. Amazon responded by
requiring its software engineers to secure more approvals for changes, and
document them better.
LLMs will continue to improve in their coding capabilities. In late 2025,
Anthropic released a new version of Claude with substantially improved
coding ability, leading the Anthropic CEO, Dario Amodei, to warn that the
capabilities of AI would lead to a bifurcated world in which the AI-entitled
would enjoy the fruits of rapid economic growth while the rest of society
suffered unemployment rates of 10% or more. This may be hype, and the
rapid growth of AI coding will be subject to checks as review by humans
ensures it is doing what is intended. One major financial firm handled such
issues by prohibiting the use of some popular generative AI tools within the
company until it could develop its own centralized generative AI platform
that allowed it to control use of the tools.
KEY IDEAS
Some errors and reliability issues with AI can be addressed by
close attention to the data curation pipeline (for RAG and fine-
tuning, in addition to pretraining).
Key components of the pipeline include character encoding,
filtering to improve data quality, and high-quality tokenization
processes.
Data ownership and control issues include the possible
inclusion of copyrighted materials in training, and potential
exposure of internal or customer data.
Summary
Warnings about the inaccuracy of AI and its tendency to hallucinate have
achieved wide currency. One contributor is the increasing prevalence of AI-
generated data on the internet, which becomes an ingredient in training
models and their resulting degradation (model collapse). A well-developed
data curation pipeline to monitor and control what goes into training can
mitigate this problem. Data scientists must also be on the lookout for some
of the negative societal risks of AI (Big Brother tendencies and copyright
infringement are two examples), even if such issues do not seem relevant
for the immediate task at hand.
Exploration with AI
Consider the Chapter 4 example in “Assessing the Model”, and the
further analysis with AI at the end of that chapter. Consider also the
likely fact that previous editions of this book (which lacked the
further AI analysis) have been included in the training material for
AI models. What effect might that have had on its output?
A marketing firm has been engaged to promote a book, and it has a
standard social media ad that it uses, plus a number of variants
with different ad copy, colors, and design elements. The firm tests
the options before launching a campaign. One of the ad variants
does better than the standard ad—249 clicks out of 1,020
exposures, versus 210 clicks out of 1,010 exposures for the
standard ad. Ask an AI to assess the results, including a test of
significance. Now review the sections on multiple testing
(“Multiple Testing”), practical significance (“Practical
significance”), and multi-arm bandits (“Multi-Arm Bandit
Algorithm” in Chapter 3), and ask the AI (or a second AI) to
critique its analysis. Does the critique cover angles raised in these
sections? If not, engage in a further conversation to see if the AI
can be led to consider these points.
Ask an AI to perform a cluster analysis of the data in loan3000.csv;
include in your prompt a request for the AI to ask you any
necessary questions before proceeding with its analysis. You will
note that the questions it asks include some that presume that you
are familiar both with the data and with cluster analysis. Answer
the questions as best you can, and then have the AI perform its
analysis. Next, ask the AI to critique its own analysis. Finally, ask a
second AI to critique the first AI’s analysis. Compare the two
critiques. Did the AI analysis include descriptions of the
characteristics of the clusters? Did it include discussion of how
separated and distinct the clusters are? Ask it how you might assess
the stability of the clusters, and how you might validate the
clusters.

About the Authors
Peter Bruce founded and grew the Institute for Statistics Education at
Statistics.com, which now offers about one hundred courses in statistics,
roughly a third of which are aimed at the data scientist. In recruiting top
authors as instructors and forging a marketing strategy to reach professional
data scientists, Peter has developed both a broad view of the target market
and his own expertise to reach it.
Andrew Bruce has over 30 years of experience in statistics and data
science in academia, government, and business. He has a PhD in statistics
from the University of Washington and has published numerous papers in
refereed journals. He has developed statistical-based solutions to a wide
range of problems faced by a variety of industries, from established
financial firms to internet startups, and offers a deep understanding of the
practice of data science.
Peter Gedeck has over 30 years of experience in scientific computing and
data science. After 20 years as a computational chemist at Novartis, he now
works as a senior data scientist at Collaborative Drug Discovery. He
specializes in the development of machine learning algorithms to predict
biological and physicochemical properties of drug candidates. Coauthor of
Machine Learning for Business Analytics, he earned a PhD in chemistry
from the University of Erlangen-Nürnberg in Germany and studied
mathematics at the Fernuniversität Hagen, Germany.
OceanofPDF.com
Colophon
The animal on the cover of AI-Assisted Statistics for Data Scientists is a
lined shore crab (Pachygrapsus crassipes), also known as a striped shore
crab. It is found along the coasts and beaches of the Pacific Ocean in North
America, Central America, Korea, and Japan. These crustaceans live under
rocks, in tidepools, and within crevices. They spend about half their time on
land, and periodically return to the water to wet their gills.
The lined shore crab is named for the green stripes on its brown-black
carapace. It has red claws and purple legs, which also have a striped or
mottled pattern. The crab generally grows to be 3–5 centimeters in size;
females are slightly smaller. Their eyes are on flexible stalks that can rotate
to give them a full field of vision as they walk.
Crabs are omnivores, feeding primarily on algae but also on mollusks,
worms, fungi, dead animals, and other crustaceans (depending on what is
available). They molt many times as they grow to adulthood, taking in
water to expand and crack open their old shell. Once this is achieved, they
spend several difficult hours getting free, and then must hide until the new
shell hardens.
Many of the animals on O’Reilly covers are endangered; all of them are
important to the world.
The cover illustration is by Karen Montgomery, based on a black-and-white
engraving from Pictorial Museum of Animated Nature. The series design is
by Edie Freedman, Ellie Volckhausen, and Karen Montgomery. The cover
fonts are Gilroy Semibold and Guardian Sans. The text font is Adobe
Minion Pro; the heading font is Adobe Myriad Condensed; and the code
font is Dalton Maag’s Ubuntu Mono.
OceanofPDF.com
